# T H E  E P I S T L E

It is not a matter of arrogance, but of veneration, most holy and equally most fortunate Men, that I do not shrink from addressing you—you whom, though unknown to me, I admire, cherish, venerate, and all but adore. For I know what manner of men you are, and I recognize what you have attained <note>original: "adepti sitis." The author is specifically addressing "Adepts," a term used in Hermetic tradition for those who have achieved the highest level of spiritual or alchemical knowledge.</note>, even though I am entirely ignorant of who you are individually. Indeed, even if I did know your identities, I would address you only by a hidden name <note>original: "ignoto nomine." This suggests that the true names of such enlightened masters are kept secret from the profane world.</note>, nor would I [present] these [writings] to you under any other title in...

<summary>In this opening of the dedicatory letter, the author humbles himself before a secret group of enlightened "Adepts," explaining that his address is born of deep respect rather than pride. He acknowledges their spiritual achievements while respecting the anonymity required by their secret tradition.</summary>

## TO

# THE READER.

*You wish me to deal sincerely with you, most sincere reader (for unless you are such a person, I have no business talking with you here); here you have <term>Basilius Valentinus</term> <note>A legendary figure in the history of chemistry, traditionally described as a 15th-century Benedictine monk, though his influential alchemical writings first appeared in the early 1600s</note>, speaking in Latin—not indeed for the elegancies of the Roman tongue <note>The author is apologizing for using "Technical" or Medieval Latin rather than the polished, artistic style of classical Roman authors like Cicero</note>, but for the usefulness of being understood—along with certain commentaries of my own. With me, he spoke only in German <note>original: "Germanicè"</note>, and without a commentator*

# TO THE READER.

*is* <note>original: "est", the conclusion of the sentence started on the preceding page</note>: *I, enticed by his promises and trusting in them, read him diligently. Whatever he proposes should be done, I have tested; I often erred, and I incurred many useless expenses. Sometimes, having attempted the same process ten times, I had no success—not through the* Author's *fault, but always (believe me, speaking candidly) through my own.*

*For* Basilius *is the* <term>Prince of Chemists</term> <note>original: "Chymicorum Princeps." In this era, "chemistry" and "alchemy" were often used interchangeably, and "Prince" signifies his status as the foremost authority in the field</note> *of all, as far as I know, the most outstanding, the most sincere, the most clear—*

<summary>The editor confesses to his own early failures and financial losses while attempting the alchemical experiments of Basilius Valentinus, asserting that these failures were due to his own errors rather than the author's instructions. He concludes by praising Valentinus as the most honest and superior authority in the chemical arts.</summary>

### TO THE READER.

...[we] have. <note>This is the conclusion of a word from the preceding page.</note> Once this has been read and understood, you possess whatever can be hoped for in chemistry <term>Chymia<gloss>In this period, "Chymia" referred to both practical chemistry and alchemy, the pursuit of transmuting metals or finding universal medicines.</gloss></term>: I promise you this regarding it; here I pledge my own word for it. 

I profess—lest you be mistaken—that I have not achieved everything myself; for who indeed could or would dare to do this? # COMMENTARY
### On the
### *Triumphal Chariot of Anti-*
### *mony by Basilius Valentinus, trans-*

Since I, Basilius Valentinus <note>a legendary figure, supposedly a 15th-century Benedictine monk, whose alchemical writings became highly influential in the 1600s</note>, live bound by religious vows to the Order of Saint Benedict, and since that state requires a certain different spirit of holiness than that by which mortals are commonly driven in this profane age; I thought it my duty, above all, at the beginning of this little book to declare what is necessary to be known by the pious Spagyrist <term>Spagyrist: an alchemist who focuses on "spagyrics," the process of separating and then recombining the essential components of a substance to create medicine</term>, who burns with an insatiable desire for this art; what he must do, and how he should cult- <note>The text cuts off here at the syllable "col-", likely leading to a word for "worship" or "cultivation" (*colat* or *colere*) on the following page.</note>

-># OF BASILIUS VALENTINUS<-

...he ought to refine <note>original: *limare*; literally to file or polish, meaning to perfect one's character or process</note>, so that he may lay the foundations of the whole matter—foundations which are stable, lest the house, shaken by the winds, should eventually crumble, and the entire building be swallowed in a foul ruin. Otherwise, if it were supported by more solid and firm principles, it would have endured for an age. This admonition seems to me, by virtue of my religious office, to be and always remain necessary; even at that time when we have all passed away, and none of those who live now—whether powerful or a humble farmer <note>original: *colonus*; a tenant or tiller of the soil</note>—shall appear before the eyes of men. For it is in the interest of mortals that these meditations survive after me, not only so that there may be honor and glory to the Divine Majesty, but also so that men may offer Him sincere obedience in all things.

->### The Triumph of Antimony<-

...and that not without cause. For what does man possess that he does not owe to his almighty Creator <note>original: *Creatori*</note>? Has He not, out of pure mercy, shared with us the eternal Word <note>original: *verbum æternum*</note> for the nourishment of the soul, and likewise promised eternal salvation? Has He not also provided food and clothing for the body, along with all those things without which the body could scarcely, or indeed not at all, survive <note>original: *subsistere*</note>?

This is so certainly true that I am convinced that an ungodly person will never truly participate in true medicine <term>vera medicina: in the context of Hermetic philosophy, this refers to a holistic healing power that requires the physician to be in a state of grace or spiritual alignment</term>, much less will they partake of the eternal heavenly bread <note>original: *panis cælestis*; likely a reference to the Eucharist or spiritual sustenance</note>.

->A 5<-

...to neglect. For since piety is effective for all things, as the sacred oracles <note>A common reference to the Holy Scriptures</note> hand down to us, and the chief exercise of piety is prayer—through which heavenly gifts are obtained from God, the Giver of all—Basilius <term>Basilius Valentinus: A legendary 15th-century alchemist, whose writings were highly influential in the development of early chemistry and medicine</term> certainly wants the mind to be lifted up toward Him, even during those operations of Chemistry <note>original: Chymiæ</note> most full of labor and weariness.

For even if prayer offered nothing else, it certainly gathers the mind into itself, calling it away from other matters, and makes it attentive to what is being done. From this, it follows that the mind reflects on many things and notices those which would surely escape it if it performed its work superficially or while distracted by various things. And so, with the help of prayer, we receive many things which we, not ungratefully, acknowledge we have obtained from God.

How often this is of use in spagyric <term>spagyric: A term used in alchemy referring to the process of separating, purifying, and recombining materials, often for the creation of medicine</term> works is known by anyone who has ever been completely devoted to this pursuit. How often does one receive in a single moment those things which they have sought for a long time and could not find, and understand them as if they were poured in from above or dictated by some guardian spirit <note>original: genio</note>?

This occurs in the solving of all riddles <note>original: ænigmatibus; in alchemical literature, "riddles" or "enigmas" refer to the cryptic and metaphorical descriptions of chemical processes intended to hide secrets from the uninitiated</note>. For if you burn with a great desire to know them, *this is prayer*; and if you stir your mind in every direction, reason through various paths, and meditate on many things, *this is co-operation*, so that the prayer is not idle or tempting God. Yet all this work of yours is in vain as long as you do not find the solution. But if you do not despair, but press on by persevering...

Contemplation follows prayer in sequence, by which I mean an accurate attention to the matter itself, under which fall these considerations to be applied at the beginning: what the circumstances of each thing are, what the material is, what <note>original: "quae". in desire, and by not ceasing from the work, at last in a moment of time the solution falls upon you; *this is revelation*, which you would not have received unless you had prayed with the highest desire, and worked by diligently applying effort; and yet you cannot perceive how, from all those things you thought—which were not the solution to the riddle—the solution itself emerged. This solution of the <term>riddle</term> <note>Latin: *aenigmatis*. ->### The Second Part of the Instruction ###<-

This contemplation, which completes the second part of our instruction, is heavenly, and must be understood through spiritual reasoning; for in no other way than through the spiritual thought of man can the circumstances and the very foundation of any thing be perceived. This contemplation is of two kinds: one is called "possible," the other "impossible." 

The latter consists of abundant thoughts that never lead to any result, nor do they reveal any tangible form of a thing that can be grasped. These are, indeed: *calcination* <note>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile matter or to oxidize it</note>, *sublimation* <note>turning a solid directly into a vapor and back to a solid to purify it</note>, *reverberation* <note>intense heating in a furnace where the flame is reflected back onto the material</note>, *circulation* <note>the continuous distillation and redistillation of a liquid in a closed vessel</note>, *putrefaction* <note>breaking down a substance through gentle, moist heat, similar to natural decay</note>, *digestion* <note>the slow application of gentle heat to mature a substance</note>, *distillation* <note>separating substances by their different boiling points</note>, *cohobation* <note>returning a distilled liquid back onto its solid remains to extract more essence</note>, *fixation* <note>making a volatile substance stable and resistant to fire</note>, and others not unlike these; all the stages of which are found, learned, perceived, and made manifest during the course of the work. From these it clearly appears what is mobile, what is fixed, and what is white, red, black, blue, or green—provided, of course, that the operation is correctly established by the craftsman. For it is possible that the operator may err and turn aside from the <term>royal road</term> <note>original: *via regia*; the direct and correct path toward alchemical perfection or the Philosopher's Stone</note>, but it is impossible for nature to err when she is correctly handled. # Basilius Valentinus

* The work of the hands requires a diligent application of oneself, but the merit of science consists in experience; <term>Anatomy</term> <note>In alchemical literature, "Anatomy" does not refer to the dissection of bodies, but to the "chemical anatomy"—the process of breaking a substance down into its fundamental components to understand its internal structure.</note> judges the difference between these two. ### The Triumph of Antimony. 17

For just as in spiritual matters the way of the Lord must be prepared; so also in these physical matters, the way must be opened and prepared, so that one does not wander from the right path, but instead travels by the most direct route toward health without deviating into errors.

<margin>4. Method of use</margin>
After the preparation, and especially after the separation of the good from the evil—which is accomplished through <term>resolution</term> <note>original: *resolutionem*; the process of dissolving or breaking a substance down into its constituent parts to release its essence</note>—one must proceed to the ***use***

---

By "use," the author understands what others call the ***dosage*** <note>original: *dosin*</note>; for what benefit is a medicine if you do not know in what quantity it should be administered, so that it might heal rather than harm or even kill? To learn this through experience alone is a task full of dangerous risks <note>original: *periculosæ plenum opus est aleæ*; literally, "a work full of dangerous dice-rolls"</note>, and yet, this entire discipline of dosages was originally discovered in no other way, even if it is now very easily passed down through words. Where a "living voice" <note>direct instruction from a teacher or master</note> is absent, it is better to be too timid than not prudent enough. Nevertheless, I can affirm this regarding <term>antimony</term> <note>a mineral substance central to alchemy; though toxic in its raw state, alchemists believed that when "purified," it became a supreme medicine</note>: when it has been properly prepared, it is as harmless a remedy as <term>cassia</term> <note>a mild herbal laxative derived from the bark of a tree</note> or <term>manna</term> <note>a sweet, dried plant sap used as a gentle medicinal cleanser</note>. All caution is required primarily regarding its use after the initial stages of preparation, in which it still retains much of its crude poisonous nature.

<summary>This page emphasizes that preparing the "way" for medicine is as vital as spiritual preparation, specifically focusing on the transition from purifying antimony to determining its proper dosage. The commentator warns that while raw antimony is toxic, a correctly prepared version is as safe as common herbal cleansers.</summary>

### Basil Valentine

...of weight, or the **dosage** <note>original: *dosin*; the specific quantity of a medicine to be taken at one time</note>; so that neither more nor less than what is required is applied. <margin>5. Usefulness and fruit.</margin>
After the medicine has been received into the body and has spread itself through all the limbs to seek out those defects against which it is applied, one must observe its ***usefulness***: for it can happen that even a medicine prepared with great care and administered in the proper weight might hinder rather than help certain diseases, appearing more like a **poison** <note>original: *venenum*</note> than a remedy. For this reason, an accurate reflection must be made regarding those things which provide benefit or aid; these should be noted down, so that...

### Basil Valentine ###

If the inner center <note>original: "centrum," referring to the internal source or "seed" of a disease</note> of the disease lies hidden, that specific kind of medicine must be administered which is capable of seeking out, grasping, and restoring that center. Otherwise, the physician’s labor will be empty and fruitless.

<margin>
How external diseases arising from internal causes ought to be treated.
</margin>
Furthermore, if a disease is external but arises from or is nourished by an internal source, it must never be driven back inward using external remedies. To do so would cause great harm and eventually lead to death. This can be understood by the analogy of a tree: if someone, while the tree is budding or flowering, were to drive the humors <term>Humors: In pre-modern medicine, these were the vital bodily fluids—such as blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—believed to govern health and temperament.</term> back into the interior from where they emerged through the earth's nourishment, it is so far from reaching the desired fruit from that flower that the tree would instead face suffocation through the violent congestion of fluids <note>original: "conjunctionem humorum," a gathering or clumping of fluids</note> that can find no exit.

<note>Vocabulary: Basil Valentine, kind of medicine, external disease, external remedies, humors, analogy of the tree, suffocation.</note>

# The Triumph of Antimony

Therefore, there is a great distinction between fresh wounds—whether inflicted by cutting, stabbing, or some other way—and old wounds, which take their origin from within; for fresh ones can be perfectly cured by external remedies alone. But in those which are nourished internally, the external application of oils, plasters, ointments, and balms will be of little use, unless the internal spring is stopped up, from which the humors <term>humors: the four vital fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—whose balance was thought to determine health</term> flow to the exterior. Once the spring has been stopped, the flow will cease, and the ailment can easily be cured even by diet alone.

It is not a matter of great skill to heal some common fresh wound, for any peasant might easily accomplish this with raw lard; but to meet all the symptoms that occur with wounds, and to dry up the source of the evil, *this is the task, this is the labor* <note>original: "hoc opus, hic labor est," a classical reference to Virgil's Aeneid, signifying a difficult challenge</note>.

<margin>Ancient physicians were also surgeons.</margin>

In these times of ours, however, they have taken on servants whom they assign to these tasks; for this reason, that most noble art has fallen into a mechanical operation of the hands. # Of Basil Valentine

—pally, because fire and air produce a certain <term>maturation<note>In alchemy, this refers to a ripening or "cooking" process where heat brings a substance to its state of perfection.</note></term>, by which a <term>transmutation<note>original: transmutatio; the fundamental alchemical process of changing one substance into another.</note></term> from water and earth can follow; and yet this too is a certain change, by which a most fragrant <term>balsam<note>original: balsamum; a healing, aromatic resin or ointment, often used by alchemists to represent a preserved or perfected state of matter.</note></term> can be produced from ill-smelling manure, and conversely, a foul-smelling manure can be produced from a most pleasant balsam. 

But you will say, why do you bring us such crude and absurd examples? The example, I admit, is taken from the <term>sheepfold<note>original: caula; the author is using a Latin wordplay here, contrasting the *caula* (stable or sheepfold) with the *aula* (court or palace) to justify his use of "low" or "earthy" metaphors.</note></term> rather than the palace; yet a prudent judge of things, investigating the matter with deeper inquiry, will easily understand what these things mean: namely, that from the highest things the lowest are made, and from the lowest the highest, just as from a medicine a poison, and from a poison a medicine; from the sweet comes the bitter, the sour, and the corrosive; and conversely, from a corrosive substance, another useful thing is made.

<summary>Valentine justifies using "crude" examples like manure and balsam to illustrate the alchemical principle of transmutation, arguing that the highest and lowest states of matter are interconnected. He explains that through natural processes, medicine can become poison and vice-versa, demonstrating the fluid nature of all substances.</summary>

# Basilius Valentinus

...[so] that fire and air produce a certain maturation <note>original: maturationem; the alchemical process of ripening or perfecting a substance through heat</note>, through which a transmutation can follow from water and earth. Indeed, this is also a kind of change by which a most fragrant balsam <note>a healing, aromatic resin</note> can be produced from foul-smelling dung, and conversely, a heavy-smelling dung can be produced from the most pleasing balsam.

But you will say: why do you offer us such crude and absurd examples? I confess, the example is taken from the sheepfold <note>Latin: *caula*</note> rather than the royal court <note>Latin: *aula*; the author uses this rhyme to emphasize that nature’s secrets are often hidden in humble or "dirty" places</note>. Nevertheless, a wise observer of nature, searching into the matter with deeper inquiry, will easily understand what these things mean: namely, that from the highest things the lowest are made, and from the lowest, the highest. Just as a poison can be made from a medicine, and a medicine from a poison; or the bitter, the sour, and the corrosive <term>corrosive|a substance that eats away or dissolves others, like a strong acid</term> from the sweet; and conversely, from a corrosive substance, another useful thing is made.

<summary>Basil Valentine explains how the four elements facilitate transmutation, arguing that the most precious substances can be derived from the most humble ones. He defends using "lowly" metaphors by explaining that in alchemy, medicine and poison, or the fragrant and the foul, are often two sides of the same coin.</summary>

—its of various kinds, visible to the intellect and endowed with spiritual knowledge, yet who cannot be touched or grasped when they wish to be, in the way that natural men are touched; and especially those who have fixed their dwelling in the elements, such as spirits of fire, lights, and other objects inhering in the other elements, and who are afterwards to be sentenced to eternal fires and thrust down into the places of hell. Regarding these, people have held various opinions, which we—along with <term>Basil<note>Referring to Basilius Valentinus, the purported 15th-century Benedictine monk and alchemist to whom this work is attributed.</note></term>—shall leave to be judged by divine knowledge, since they are unknown to us. 

But as for what he adds concerning the admirable force and power of <term>antimony<note>original: "antimonii". In alchemical theory, the "spirit" of antimony refers to its volatile, active essence that could be extracted through distillation.</note></term>, or of other chemical spirits—which we ourselves have seen so often with the highest admiration—we shall understand this as referring only to material spirits. These are certainly of such great strength, and they effect such marvelous things, that I cannot remember anything greater or more wonderful ever being committed to writing about those spirits which certain dreamers—whose imaginations are perverted by <term>melancholy<note>original: "melancholiâ". In early modern medicine, an excess of "black bile" (melancholy) was thought to cause delusions, hallucinations, and false visions.</note></term>—falsely claim to see and converse with; nor have I heard such things told by those who, with a license for lying, try either to terrify or delight others.

<summary>The author distinguishes between supernatural spirits of the elements and "material spirits" found in alchemy, arguing that the chemical power of antimony is more wondrous than the hallucinations of the mentally ill.</summary>

->### Of Basilius Valentinus ###<-

...shining streaks; and therefore one variety is more suitable than the other for use in both **Medicine** and **Alchemy**. This is much like comparing the flesh of a fish to the flesh of other animals: although both are and are called "flesh," there is nevertheless a great difference between them. The same logic applies to the nature of <term>antimony</term> <note>original: "antimonii"; a metallic mineral that Valentinus considered the "prime" subject for both medical cures and the transformation of metals.</note>.

Many indeed write about the <term>inner virtue</term> <note>In alchemy, "virtue" (virtus) refers to the inherent medicinal or spiritual power trapped within a raw substance.</note> of antimony, but few of them have either taught the true foundation of the powers with which it is endowed, or discovered by what reason or in what manner it received those powers. Consequently, their doctrine is founded only on words and stops short of the true foundation; thus, they lose the fruit which they hoped to attain through their writing.

To write truly about antimony requires profound meditation and a mind that expands itself widely—

Antimony <term>antimony: a metallic mineral often found as stibnite, used by alchemists to "purify" gold and as a base for powerful medicines</term> has this in common with mercury <note>original: *mercurio*; the archetypal fluid and transformative principle in alchemy</note>: it may be compared to a **round circle**, which has no end, and which is composed of all colors. In this substance, one always finds more the more diligently one searches, provided that one proceeds by the right path and in the proper order. It is a **poison**, even a most immediate poison, yet it is also free from poison and a most excellent medicine, whether applied externally or internally

# The Triumph of Antimony

...be applied; a fact which remains hidden to so many because of their blindness. They judge it to be incredible, foolish, and vain—an attitude for which they should be forgiven on account of their lack of skill. One can grant them the pardon they deserve for their sheer stupidity; however, what cannot be tolerated is that they have no desire to be taught or to be better informed, neither in this matter nor in any other.

<margin>Antimony is endowed with all four primary qualities.</margin>
Antimony is endowed with all four primary qualities <note>original: "quatuor primis qualitatibus"; referring to the Aristotelian system of Hot, Cold, Moist, and Dry which governed all matter in medieval science.</note>: it is cold and moist, and yet also hot and dry. It adapts itself to the four seasons of the year. Likewise, it is both volatile <term>volatile: in alchemy, this describes a substance that easily vaporizes or "escapes" when heated.</term> and fixed <term>fixed: describes a substance that remains stable and solid even under intense heat.</term>. Its volatile part is not free from poison; its fixed part, by contrast, is entirely free of all toxicity. This is so wondrous that antimony is considered one of the seven wonders of the world. So many authors have written about it, though they themselves did not know what they were writing...

<summary>This page describes antimony as a miraculous substance containing all four primary Aristotelian qualities and both volatile and fixed states. The author criticizes ignorant scholars who dismiss these properties out of a refusal to learn.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of his expertise regarding antimony, following his critique of previous authors who wrote without true understanding of the substance's qualities.</meta>

### Basil Valentine

...brought forth. <margin>* Here he pours himself out in praises of antimony. Read, read, O Friend of Chemistry <note>original: "Philochymice"; a Greek-derived term for a lover of alchemy or "chemical friend."</note>; you will find nothing hyperbolic, nothing boastful <note>original: "Thrasonicum," referring to Thraso, a bragging soldier in Roman comedy.</note>. Basil will not exhaust its praises by speaking, because no one until now has been able to know all its powers through experience. We have seen much, and much that is new is found daily by curious searchers <note>original: "scrutatoribus."</note>, yet more remains to be found. Indeed, just as it is an inexhaustible fountain in the fire (for the more you extract from it, the more it gives; for if you were to extract vinegar <note>Refers to "Philosophical Vinegar" or "Vinegar of Antimony," a potent acidic liquid alchemists extracted from the mineral.</note> from it a thousand times, a thousand times it will give something new, without the addition of any other substance), so it is an inestimable treasure of virtues, forever new. Nature seems to have chosen this mineral as the place to hide all her treasures, so that not without merit has **Basil** constructed for it a **triumphal chariot**, which is daily enriched with new spoils snatched from the camps of ignorance.</margin> No one until now has existed before me, and today no one is found, who has finally so thoroughly learned its power, virtues, strengths, operation, and efficacy, and has so profoundly penetrated its every energy, that nothing more remains hidden within it to be discovered which cannot be proven through experience.

# Basil Valentine
## Page 58

<margin>Application of the example.</margin>
Here I warn you, whoever you are, who desire to be instructed by my writings, and hope to obtain riches and true medicine from <term>antimony<note>A metallic element that was central to Valentine's alchemical work; he believed it held the key to both medical cures and the transmutation of metals.</note></term>, that you do not run through this my intention superficially. There is not a single letter in it which has been written in vain, and which does not have some singular significance for your instruction. Indeed, I here affirm once and for all, that many words are found scattered here and there in my writings, to which if the reader would attend in the proper manner, and would recognize upon which foundations the principal points of the matter are situated, and in a way buried, he would not regret reading them more often—

---

—if you do not arrive with such certain <term>guidance<note>original: *manuductione*; literally "a leading by the hand," suggesting a practical, step-by-step apprenticeship.</note></term>, I do not know what <term>staff<note>original: *scipio*; a staff or walking stick.</note></term> will be given to the lame, or what demonstration of the journey will direct the one who is dazed. Believe, read, meditate, work: and spare yourself so many books of the <term>Chemists<note>original: *Chymicorum*; referring here to other alchemical authors whose works Valentine considers distracting or erroneous.</note></term> which distract you with various errors of the paths; this one book says everything.

Basil indulges his temperament somewhat here by attacking the **Pseudo-doctors**, whose ignorance in those times was so great that they despised every more subtle preparation of medicine which he and the **Chymists** <term>Chymists: a term used for early practitioners of chemistry and alchemy who sought to purify substances through fire and distillation</term> with him professed. # Basil Valentine

...medicine, and they are utterly ignorant of how a poison must be prepared so that it might pass into a wholesome medicine, and in place of its malignancy, take on a better nature. Against these I am the first to raise my voice and protest; against those, I say, who—ignorant of the art of preparation—proffer poisons to men. For indeed **mercury** <term>Mercury: also known as quicksilver, one of the three primary principles in alchemy alongside sulfur and salt</term>, **orpiment** <term>Orpiment: a yellow arsenic sulfide mineral (Latin: auripigmentum) used as a pigment and in chemical experiments</term>, **antimony**, and others similar to these are poisons in their own substance, and unless they are correctly prepared, they remain poisons.

<margin>Poison rightly prepared drives out poison.</margin>
Yet after a legitimate preparation <term>Preparation: the alchemical process of refining, purifying, or altering a substance, usually through heat or chemical reaction, to remove its harmful traits</term>, all poisonous quality is broken, extinguished, and expelled; so that nothing remains of them except a medicine which resists all internal poisons, even those most deeply rooted, and plucks them out from the very foundations. For a poison prepared in such a manner, so that it can no longer cause harm...

<summary>Basil Valentine critiques physicians who do not understand how to chemically transform toxic substances like mercury and antimony into safe, effective medicines. He argues that proper alchemical preparation "extinguishes" the poison, leaving only a powerful curative agent.</summary>

* Poets not infrequently place the beginning of their story or fable in the middle of their poem, so that by perpetual suspense they may more easily achieve their goal, which is to delight their readers. <margin>Various names for Antimony.</margin>

# The Triumph of Antimony

From the diversity of opinions, it must first be gathered by a more prudent judgment that <term>antimony</term><note>original: *antimonium*. A brittle, silvery-white metalloid that was a cornerstone of alchemical medicine, believed by many to be a "universal medicine" if properly prepared.</note> was known and highly valued by the Arabs, the Chaldeans, the Latins, and our own Germans. <margin>An example of soap.</margin>
A very clear example of this principle is found in <term>soap<note>Latin: *sapone*. The author is using a common chemical process—saponification—to explain a complex alchemical theory of transmutation.</note></term> or cleansing-agent. It is composed of oil and other fatty ingredients, which seem more suitable for staining linen than for cleaning it—and in truth, they are; but because a certain separation and preparation has been achieved through the boiling of the soap during its cooking, primarily by the aid of salt, the soap is rendered most fit to draw toward itself and wash away all fat and filth from linen and other things. In the same way, truly, a poison can be adapted through a specific prior preparation so that it is no longer a poison, but becomes a **medicine**, and may draw another poison to itself, cast it out, and restore a person to their original purity and health.

# The Triumph of Antimony

# The Triumph of Antimony

<margin>The star of the sun <note>original: "astrum solis"; in alchemy, the "star" refers to the internal celestial virtue or "seed" of a substance</note> surpasses every medicine.</margin>

Nevertheless, since the **star of the sun**—from which, as if from an active and all-enlivening sun, all things in their kind universally arise—surpasses all others, I will permit nature, working above all things, to act through the power of **gold**, that is, in its own "star." It is from this source that gold itself, and indeed all metals and minerals, received their first birth and the propagation of their generation in the beginning. More can be said of this when I reveal the star of the sun to you and commend it most solemnly to your conscience.

The same reasoning must also be applied to **antimony**, which possesses the same operations as physical gold, though I am not speaking of its "star" at this moment. "For I know," says Antimony, "that I must be struck with awe, fear, and trembling before it, although in..."

# The Triumph of Antimony

<margin>Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire and smithing; in alchemy, he represents the transformative power of the furnace and the alchemist's skill in governing heat</note> is the judge of all things.</margin>

This right belongs to Vulcan alone, so that he may boast of being the master in his own element of fire. This can be demonstrated as a true and manifest proof through the following example: When the hardest steel <term>chalybs: a term for hardened iron or steel, signifying strength and resistance</term> is struck by a hard, solid flint, fire arouses fire through violent motion. This kindling draws forth the hidden sulfur—or rather, the hidden fire is made manifest by that violent agitation and is ignited through the air, so that it burns truly and effectively. The salt remains in the ash, and the mercury rushes forth from it along with the burning sulfur.

So, in this case, understand that antimony <term>antimonium: a metallic element that Basil Valentine championed as a source of powerful, albeit dangerous, medicines once purified</term> must be grasped in such a way that its mercury is separated from its sulfur and salt in a natural manner. Now, just as fire, which lies hidden and concealed within matter, can achieve nothing useful—nothing, so to speak, tangible to the hands, nothing great—unless it is made manifest and can be demonstrated; so too, medicine will accomplish nothing remarkable unless it is first separated from the coarse <note>original: "crasso," referring to the "crude" or unrefined physical body of the substance</note> matter, rectified, and thus disburdened—

To summarize many points in few words, I say that this is the condition of antimony <term>antimony: A brittle, silvery-white metalloid that served as a cornerstone of alchemical study, believed to be a "lead" of the philosophers.</term>. Whatever is hidden and concealed from common knowledge enjoys the name and honor of "Art" <term>Art: In alchemy, 'Art' (Ars) refers to the specialized knowledge and spiritual discipline required to perfect nature.</term> as long as it remains secret. But as soon as that which was hidden is made manifest, the Art reaches its conclusion and passes into the realm of manual labor <note>original: "opus mechanicum"</note>—a point I have made more than once in my other writings.

# Of Basil Valentine

<margin>*From honey, a most terrible corrosive can be made.*</margin>
[The bee] flits <note>original: "fugit"</note> from the flowers by that art which the Almighty has instilled in it, in which there lies hidden power, juice, and a strengthening force <note>original: "vis roborativa"</note>, from which a medicine visible to all eyes is prepared. Now, truly, from that sweet and most pleasant-tasting honey, a most terrible corrosive <term>corrosive: In alchemy, a powerful acid or caustic substance, often used to dissolve metals, which can be deadly if handled without skill.</term> and an immediate poison can be prepared; which perhaps no one would believe, unless he learned it by experience; certainly no one, except for the most diligent observer, would have noticed it.

For this reason, however, honey must not be despised, nor should it be said—despite its most pleasant sweetness—that it is a corruption of medicine simply because a corrosive can be made from it. Instead, one must say that this outcome arises from the lack of skill <note>original: "imperitia"</note> of the Physician, who does not know how to prepare it in the proper manner. Here I now have something to teach the unskilled Physician, so that he may be liberated at the final judgment <note>original: "liberationem ab ultimo judicio"; this refers to the author's belief that doctors are spiritually accountable for the harm they cause through ignorance.</note>.

### The Triumph of Antimony. 93

Honey is prepared from the waste products of brute animals, by which richness is supplied to the fields and plains. In these places grow various kinds of flowers, herbs, and shrubs, from which bees suck out the most noble part of the <term>quintessence</term> <note>original: *quintæ essentiæ*; in alchemy, the "fifth essence" or the purest, most potent spiritual core of a substance</note>. Through this process, an alteration and generation of one thing into another occurs—namely, into a food of a different form and taste, which in no way resembles the original materials, and is called honey. From that honey, a food is prepared for man that is pleasant, timely, most welcome, and useful for many purposes. From that same honey, however, a poison can be prepared for both men and cattle that is harmful in the highest degree.

Therefore, you, <term>investigator of nature</term> <note>original: *naturæ perscrutator*</note>, of whatever age, sex, fortune, or level of education you may be: follow me and follow nature; I shall [lead] you to the truth without any false-

### The Triumph of Antimony

...so that later descendants may record it in an indelible book.

Every Physician must, above all else, take care that while he rushes to and fro to restore lost health, he does not institute a treatment contrary to nature, nor turn aside from the right intention. When spirit of wine <term>spiritus vini: high-proof alcohol or ethanol</term> is poured onto aqua fortis <term>strong water: nitric acid</term>, a violent boiling <note>original: "ebullitio"; likely referring to the vigorous exothermic reaction between alcohol and acid</note> occurs, and these two natures do not easily tolerate one another. In the same way, oil or liquor of tartar <note>a solution of potassium carbonate</note> and vinegar made from noble wine are compared to one another: for they hate each other, and no less than fire avoids water, one flees the other—even though they have both come from the same material <note>Both tartar and vinegar are byproducts of the winemaking process</note>. ### The Triumph of Antimony

<sig>E 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: beginning (original: "cipio," from "principio")</meta>

...I lead, nor am I able, nor do I have the power to do so.

<margin>The origin of antimony.</margin>
To write about antimony and begin from its very foundation—from which antimony acquires its power, triumph, and glory, and through which it is raised to its most perfect function—we must first reveal the true origin of its roots. We must explore how it is born within the earth, which stars govern it, and which elements have "cooked" it <note>original: "percoxerint," referring to the alchemical concept of subterranean heat ripening minerals like fruit</note> and brought it to maturity. Antimony, therefore, is nothing other than a smoke or (to put it another way) a mineral vapor. This vapor is created by the stars above and then, through the action of the elements, is refined and "cooked" until it reaches a solid form <note>original: "formalem coagulationem"</note> and maturity. <margin>Antimony and mercury arise from the same origin.</margin> It is important to note that from the beginning, antimony acquired its essence, virtue, power, function, and quality from the same celestial body <note>original: "astro," referring to a specific star or planet</note>...

<summary>This page explains the alchemical origins of antimony, describing it as a "mineral vapor" produced by celestial influences and matured by the elements. It establishes that antimony and mercury share the same cosmic source.</summary>

<margin>Definition of Antimony.</margin>
Antimony, therefore, is a mineral made from the vapor of the earth changed into water, which is a spiritual, sidereal <note>original: *syderea*; this refers to the alchemical belief that minerals are formed through the influence of the stars and planets</note> transformation, <margin>The Star of Antimony.</margin> the true star of antimony. This water is extracted by the stars from the elementary earth, then through the element of fire—which resides in the air—and through coagulation is formally changed into a tangible essence. In this tangible essence (from which antimony is formally produced) there is found a great abundance of predominating **sulfur** <term>Sulfur: In alchemy, this is one of the "Three Principles," representing the "soul," internal heat, and the ability to burn.</term>; after this, **mercury** <term>Mercury: The second principle, representing the "spirit," fluidity, and volatility.</term> is reckoned; however, of the three principles, there is the least of **salt** <term>Salt: The third principle, representing the "body," solidity, and the physical structure of a substance.</term>. Yet it has taken on just enough salt that it has consequently attained its hard and unmalleable form.

<margin>Qualities of Antimony.</margin>

# The Triumph of Antimony

This widespread outcry has so influenced most people that, even until these times of mine, <term>antimony</term> <note>Latin: *antimonium*. A metallic element that was the subject of intense medical debate in the 17th century; it was often toxic if poorly prepared but hailed as a miracle cure by alchemists.</note> has a bad reputation, and no one trusts its medicinal power, which is found within it in such varied and inexplicable ways.

<margin>Prepared antimony is the highest medicine.</margin>
For I truly and solemnly affirm—as surely as God is the creator of all visible things contained in either heaven or earth, and of all creatures which have either come to our knowledge or are yet to come—that no greater medicine under heaven and the rays of the sun will ever be demonstrated with experience as a guide. In no other substance will such a pinnacle of health be found so truly and so abundantly as will be declared to be within antimony through certain and <term>undeniable experiments</term> <note>Original: *irrefragabilibus experimentis*. The author emphasizes that his knowledge comes from practical laboratory work, not just theory.</note>.

Hear my speech, my son, and you, reader, pay close attention to my writings.

### The Triumph of Antimony. 117

...if you whisper and grumble against me, listen for a moment, and while I am by no means unmindful of your argument, hear what I shall say to you here and now.

<margin>Antimony is not to be rejected because it is a poison.</margin>

Should no mortal use it then, because there is poison in it? 

<margin>99</margin>
Does this logic please you? Does this teaching of mine satisfy you? You have heard that after its preparation, no toxicity is found in antimony; for by the spagyric art <term>Spagyric: An alchemical method of medicine-making that involves "separating" a substance into its components, purifying them, and "recombining" them into a more potent form.</term> it is necessary that antimony be converted from a poison into a medicine, just as...

## Of Basilius Valentinus

In alchemy, "Vulcan" is a personification of the fire used in the laboratory furnace to transform and purify matter.</term>; for he is the master and revealer of all **secrets** <note>original Latin: *arcanorum*</note>. ->The Triumph of Antimony. 119<-

Now I shall arrive at the processes and the preparation of antimony <term>antimony: a brittle, silvery-white metalloid that alchemists believed held the secret to the "Universal Medicine"</term>; hereafter, I shall hold in disregard <note>original: "sus deque habebo," a Latin idiom meaning to be indifferent to or treat as of no consequence</note> those outcries of arrogant men, who applaud only themselves. ### The Triumph of Antimony

...can such a mineral be used safely and without danger for health after the <term>evacuation of poison|The alchemical process of "stripping" or purifying a raw substance to remove its toxic or "crude" properties</term>? To these questions, I wish to give a brief and clear answer. The infusion of poison must be considered in two ways: the natural and the supernatural.

The first reason why God—the supreme Lord of the stars and the Creator of heaven and earth—presented us with open poison, especially within minerals, is so that by this decree He might show us His wonderful and powerful works. This allows for the distinction between good and evil, just as He prescribed for us in the Law <note>original: "in lege"</note> the knowledge of what a person is required to do: namely, to embrace the good and flee from the evil.

In the same way, the Tree of Life was offered in Paradise; its proper use resulted in good;

For God does not wish for man to be drowned in his own ruin and perish, but rather that he should turn away from evil and move toward <term>improvement|Latin: *emendatio*; in this context, it suggests a process of refining or "correcting" a substance or soul to its ideal state</term>, by which destruction might be driven from his soul. Thus, with wonderful suitability, He set before us—His creatures—both good and evil, which is found both in the commandment of His Word and in the <note>original: "opere creaturæ"</note> physical works of His creation. This was done so that we might choose what is useful and good for health, and avoid what is evil and destructive.

### Basilius Valentinus

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;in this manner: whatever is contrary to nature is a poison; and this is so because it is contrary to it. Just as when someone eats a food that their stomach cannot bear, then that food is a poison to them; for it is contrary to their nature. Conversely, if there is someone for whom this food is agreeable <note>original: "amicus," literally "friendly," meaning compatible with the individual's specific constitution</note>, then that very food is a medicine to them.

### Basilius Valentine ###

[...vegetable things] they may be, they are still poisonous, because a certain **volatile mercurial matter** <note>original: "mercurialis quædam volatilis materia"; in alchemy, "Mercury" represents the principle of volatility and spirit. If it is not "fixed" by heat, it remains restless and can be toxic.</note> still dominates in them. This volatile spirit is the reason why other things within a person are expelled <note>Meaning they act as "purgatives" that cause vomiting or diarrhea.</note>; it is not that the very root of the disease is seized by this means, for that is something only achieved through the **fixity** <note>original: "fixitatem"; the state where a substance has been stabilized by fire so it no longer evaporates or causes violent reactions.</note> of a substance. 

<margin>Fixed medicines attack the root of diseases.</margin>

For every medicine that has been brought to a state of **fixity** seeks out fixed diseases and tears them out by the roots. This is something that non-fixed **purgatives** <note>original: "purgantia"; substances used to cleanse the bowels or stomach.</note> cannot do; instead, they merely carry away a sort of "spoil" <note>original: "spolium"; Valentine suggests they only take away the surface-level waste produced by the disease, not the cause itself.</note> or surface-level waste from the disease. They might be compared to water driven with force down a street, which washes the surface but otherwise does not soak into the earth itself. 

On the other hand, things that have been rendered **fixed** do not work by purging through the lower parts—a method common to poisonous, non-fixed substances—and therefore diseases, if I may put it this way, [at their] core <note>The Latin text cuts off at "nu-", which the catchword "cleum" identifies as "nucleum" (kernel or core).</note>...

<margin>What may be found in antimony.</margin>
Oh, the sharpness of the worldly wit of those who neither understand these writings of mine, nor bestow any effort toward understanding them! If you had known what is called "fixed" and what is called "unfixed," and what it signifies to separate the pure from the impure, you would surely have consigned many of your affairs to oblivion; and, having abandoned other insane labors, you would follow me alone. <term>Mercury</term> <note>In alchemy, the "spirit" or fluid principle of a substance</note> is in the <term>regulus</term> <note>original: "regulo"; the metallic heart or "little king" found at the bottom of the crucible after smelting</note>, <term>sulfur</term> <note>The "soul" or combustible principle, often associated with color</note> is in the red color, and <term>salt</term> <note>The "body" or stabilizing principle</note> is in the discarded black earth. # Basilius Valentinus

...various colors is attributed to Jupiter <note>The alchemical name for Tin</note>. Just as all the colors of all metals and precious stones are clearly found in antimony, so also are all the powers and virtues of medicine shown within it, no less than its colors. However, to draw out all those colors from it is not the work of a single man; for life is bounded by narrower limits than would allow one person to learn, through their own labor, everything that Nature carries hidden and concealed within her bosom. 

<margin>Medicines of every kind are made from antimony.</margin>

For it happens by one method that, through preparation, an acidic liquid is drawn from antimony by distillations, much like a true and pure vinegar. By another path, a transparent red color is prepared, sweet and flavorful like clarified honey or sugar. At other times, a bitterness comes forth like that of wormwood <note>original: "absynthii"; a plant known for its extreme bitterness, used here to describe the flavor of an alchemical extract</note>. At other times, an acrimony <note>A sharp, biting, or pungent quality</note> which—

Antimony now provides so many extractions, and variegated with so many colors, that it could weary Delphic Apollo <note>In Greek mythology, Apollo was the god of prophecy and truth; the author suggests the variety of antimony is so vast it would exhaust even a god to explain it all.</note>, if indeed he wished to reveal to us through his oracles all the mutations of its nature which are brought back through the gate of fire <note>A poetic term for the alchemical furnace or the process of calcination and heating.</note>.

From it is made quicksilver <note>original: "mercurius vivus"</note>; from it is made sulfur, which burns just like common sulfur—so much so that gunpowder <note>original: "pulvis pyrius"</note> can be prepared from it; from it is made a true and natural salt; and many other things are made from it.

Let us therefore proceed and speak of its preparations, such as its **essence**, **magistery**, **arcanum**, **elixir**, and **particular tincture** <note>These terms represent different grades of alchemical refinement and concentration, moving from basic extracts to the most potent spiritual essence of the mineral.</note>; to which you shall finally apply yourself with all diligence and zeal, once I have described to you in my writings the stone which is called ***fire***, and its preparation.

# The Triumph of Antimony

->The Triumph of Antimony<-

...I shall declare, together with various secrets and mysteries <note>original: *arcanis*</note> that are scarcely, or not at all, known to the world, and to which little attention is paid since the Egyptians, Arabs, and Chaldeans—who once practiced these arts—have passed away. Yet their methods are of the greatest use for searching out the true source of true medicine and for completing its other works.

Pay attention now, therefore, and reflect with deep meditation that all the preparations follow one after another, just as I shall reveal them to you; for none will be introduced that does not have its own use, but each is arranged according to its proper state. For the <term>fixed</term> <note>In alchemy, a "fixed" substance is one that has been processed to withstand the heat of fire without vaporizing, making it more potent and stable as a medicine.</note> medicine of antimony expels fixed and deep-rooted illnesses; however, that which is not fixed—such as crude and unprepared antimony—only opens and purges the belly...

## 1st. Calcination. <term>Calcination: A foundational alchemical process involving the heating of a substance—such as a metal or mineral—at high temperatures to drive off moisture or volatile components, eventually reducing the material to a fine, dry powder called a "calx."</term>

-># The Triumph of Antimony. 137<-

...consists, and it is performed in this manner.

<margin>The calcination of antimony.</margin>
Take the best Hungarian antimony <note>original: "antimonium Hungaricum." Hungarian antimony was highly prized by alchemists for its perceived purity and high quality</note>, or whatever other kind you are able to obtain; grind it into a powder that is, if possible, impalpable <note>so fine that the individual grains cannot be felt by the touch</note>. Spread this powder gently and thinly across a round or square potter's dish <note>original: "scutella figularia"</note>, which should be surrounded by a raised rim about two fingers wide. Place this dish into a calcining furnace <term>calcining furnace: a specialized oven used to heat minerals to high temperatures to drive off volatile impurities without melting the material</term>; at the start, apply a moderate fire made from charcoal. When you see smoke rising from the antimony, stir it with an iron spatula <note>original: "spathâ ferreâ"</note>, never stopping as long as it emits any smoke. If during the calcination the antimony reaches a state where it begins to form into balls or lumps, remove it from the fire. When it has cooled, grind it again into a fine powder, and having placed it back on the fire, stir and calcine it as was done before, until no more smoke...

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the medicinal use of antimony, specifically addressing the dangers of its misuse and proposing a safer preparation known as the 'glass of antimony.'</meta>

### The Triumph of Antimony

<margin>But</margin>
violent vomiting and <term>excessive purging</term> <note>original: "supercatharticas evacuationes"</note> cause the soul to suddenly expire; and hence those tears, hence those outcries arise against the Chemists, as if the impious recklessness of certain **pseudo-chemists** <note>original: "Pseudochymicorum", referring to fraudulent or unskilled alchemists who gave the art a bad name</note> should be blamed on the art itself. It matters nothing to these men how many homes they fill with funerals, provided that one or two of their cured patients trumpet their fame, and they are heard of as great Doctors, and swindle money from the peasants. The reason for this danger is that all the <term>emetic power</term> <note>original: "vis emetica", the property that induces vomiting</note> of antimony is contained in its **fixed salt**, in which salt all its poisonous quality dwells at the same time. Weak constitutions cannot overcome this, and hence they do not receive as much benefit from its healing power as they receive harm from the poison. But this matter should not deter sensible people from the use of antimony, since they see that even then, when the poison is mixed in, it often produces such health-giving effects. Therefore, one must rather reason thus: if that healing power were liberated from the harmful faculty, what good would it not accomplish? What diseases would it not heal? Behold, therefore, for you, the **glass of antimony**, which I use often and which can be brought into use by anyone without any danger of a fatal catastrophe.

*Take pure **glass of antimony** <note>original: "vitrum antimonii", a transparent, amber-colored substance produced by roasting and melting antimony ore</note>, prepared as **Basilius** <note>likely referring to Basilius Valentinus, the legendary alchemist and author of "The Triumphal Chariot of Antimony"</note> teaches here: melt it in a crucible so that it flows liquidly until a third part is consumed. Grind this into an impalpable powder...*

# Basil Valentine

...pure antimony: since others are prepared by the addition of borax and other substances in this manner.

<margin>
Glass of antimony with addition.
</margin>

*Take one part of crude antimony and two parts of Venetian **borax** <note>original: "boracis Veneta", a flux used in chemistry to help substances melt and to purify them</note>; grind these together. Pour over them the highest quality rectified **spirit of wine** <note>original: "spiritum vini", a term for distilled alcohol or ethanol</note>, so that it stands three finger-widths above the powder. Seal the vessel tightly and let it circulate for three months. Extract the spirit of wine by distillation, or if it has been tinted with a red color (which will always happen if you have proceeded correctly in your operation), simply pour it off and set it aside, for it is an excellent medicine. Place the remaining solid mass back into a crucible, allow it to melt, and pour it into any molds you wish; for it can take on any shape you desire, even of faces, which may be set into rings and worn on the hand. The use, however, is this:*

*Place this glass into two ounces of cold wine overnight, and in the morning let the sick person drink that wine; you will see the best successes. For it purges gently, and if nature is inclined toward the **offending matter** <note>original: "materia peccans", the perceived harmful substance or humor causing illness in early modern medicine</note> being expelled from above, it follows that lead softly and induces gentle vomiting. Note only this: the prescribed dose must be reduced according to the strength, age, and constitution of the sick person. And this first <term>treasure</term> <note>original: "cimelion", a Greek-derived word for a precious object or heirloom kept in a treasury</note> offered to you by me, reader, set aside; and expect more, if you read the rest with gratitude and attention.*

<summary>This page provides a detailed recipe for preparing a medicinal 'glass of antimony' using borax and spirit of wine, emphasizing its gentle purgative and emetic effects when infused in wine.</summary>

With all this precaution of mine, I still could not prevent myself from laboring for an entire year, repeating this tincture many times always in a failed attempt, so that a weariness for almost all of **Chemistry** <note>original: "Chymiæ"</note> overcame me out of despair: for my tincture was of no efficacy in **Medicine**, because it was only a **dead head** <note>original: "caput mortuum"; the worthless, inert dregs left over after a chemical process</note> and tasteless. Learn from this how little it matters to compile some process from a written text, or even to receive it by word of mouth from a friend, unless you are somehow led by the hand and taught the smallest **manual techniques** <note>original: "encherefes," from the Greek "encheiresis"; practical, hands-on laboratory maneuvers</note> that must be observed during the work. At the same time, see how generously I deal with you, while I reveal that which cost me such great trouble and expense in my ignorance.

For if it eludes you, the matter is finished, the whole operation is lost, and you will acquire nothing but a dead residue: for as soon as the drops...

---

When twenty, or at most thirty, measures of sweet water have come forth, acidity appears once again. Those who are either inexperienced in this matter or careless, thinking this is the acidity of the vinegar, continue with the distillation, waiting for the water to become sweet again. But since this is the specific acidity of the antimony itself—which immediately follows the sweet water once the vinegar has been extracted—it happens that by persisting in the distillation, all the virtue <note>The "virtue" refers to the medicinal or chemical potency of the substance.</note> of the antimony is destroyed. What remains is a <term>caput mortuum<note>Latin for "dead head"; the useless, inert solid residue left after all volatile components have been driven off.</note></term> that is both useless and tasteless. 

You, therefore, must be more cautious: when this sweet water comes forth, remove the pulp that is at the bottom, place it into another glass vessel, and allow it to evaporate in the heat of the Sun or another very moderate heat, so that a dry powder is left behind. Once you have escaped this <term>Charybdis<note>A reference to the mythical Greek whirlpool; here used to mean a dangerous technical pitfall that could ruin the work.</note></term>, proceed. *See above.

However, these should not be administered unless the body has first been cleared of the <term>ballast of offending humors<note>original: "saburrâ humorum peccantium." In the medical theory of the time, "humors" were bodily fluids that caused disease if they became stagnant or imbalanced.</note></term>. Otherwise, you would be throwing these gems onto a <term>dunghill<note>original: "sterquilinium"</note></term>, where, buried under filth, they would be unable to shine or demonstrate their powers. For although they might be called "universal" remedies due to their many virtues, they are still to be categorized as specific treatments. According to the opinion of **Galen** <note>The famous 2nd-century Greek physician whose theories dominated European medicine for over a millennium.</note> and almost all physicians everywhere, general treatments must always be administered before specific ones.

The Tincture discussed here achieves everything that **Basil** <note>Referring to Basilius Valentinus, the reputed author of "The Triumphal Chariot of Antimony."</note> enumerates, provided its use is continued for some time. For if that saying "medicines applied help, but continued they heal" holds true anywhere, it must certainly be applied to those remedies that work imperceptibly.

<summary>This page describes the proper administration of antimonial tinctures, emphasizing that the body must be purged of "offending humors" before these powerful alchemical remedies can be effective. It reconciles the alchemical teachings of Basil Valentine with the traditional medical authority of Galen.</summary>

### Basil Valentine

*...once this is done, let it be placed* \* *in eggs boiled to hardness* <note>hard-boiled eggs</note>, *in the place of the yolk which has been removed; and let it be placed in a damp place, or a cellar, and the powder will be dissolved into a yellow liquor.* This wonderful liquor <margin>Healing Powers</margin> heals all recent wounds, if at the start it is applied to the wound with a soft feather, and the wound is bound with a <term>styptic<note>original: "stiptico." A substance used to stop bleeding by contracting the skin or blood vessels.</note></term> or other protective plaster. All recent wounds, whether inflicted by *stabbing or by cutting* <margin>127</margin>, are healed by this liquor without rot, inflammation, or any excess of pus. This is achieved so perfectly that praise and thanks should rightly be returned to Him who created heaven and earth and gave such medicine to mankind.

> \* Soft eggs, as they say, should always be handled carefully and softly; but even with these hard-boiled ones, there is something I must warn you about. # The Triumph of Antimony

In all old, malignant, and corrosive wounds, use this extraction, or **balsam of antimony** <note>A medicinal preparation derived from antimony, a semi-metal that alchemists believed could "purge" the body of disease just as it was used to "purge" gold of impurities.</note>. It will never fail you in your time of need, and you yourself, following after me, will join in praising its virtues and proclaim how it blesses mortals when applied externally; [it is effective against] the "wolf" <note>original: "lupus"; a historical term for a consuming or "eating" skin ulcer, often associated today with systemic lupus or various necrotic infections.</note> and * cancer.

* <note>If surgeons placed their trust in our author here, with what great zeal they would prepare this balsam for themselves, and with what profit and frequency they would use it! But I give my own word that **Basil** <note>original: "Basilius"; referring to Basil Valentine, the legendary 15th-century alchemical author.</note> actually fell far short in his praise, for it accomplishes more than he even claims for it. A single short story taken from the hundreds of my own medical observations will confirm this. A woman in her forties had suffered great pain in her left breast for seven whole years, which was accompanied by a hard tumor. All the surgeons and physicians she had consulted judged with one voice that she was suffering from cancer—and indeed she was, even in the judgment of that famous practitioner who, at *Oirschot* <note>original: "Orschoti"; a village in the Netherlands.</note> (which is a village near *'s-Hertogenbosch* <note>original: "Sylvam Ducis"; a city in the southern Netherlands.</note>), quite laudably and indeed successfully [continues to next page...]</note>

# Basil Valentine

...successfully <note>original: "feliciter"; completing the sentence from the previous page regarding a famous practitioner.</note> yield to it, as do decay in the bones, malignant ulcers, and those caused by worms.

[This practitioner] is successfully engaged in surgery and draws a huge crowd of people to himself; for after he had attempted to treat this disease for three months, he finally sternly pronounced that it could not be rooted out except by the removal of the limb <note>original: "membri ablatione"; though the tumor was in the breast, the term refers generally to the surgical removal of a body part.</note>. The woman, stubborn to endure all the pains of the disease rather than submit to such a fatal and risky remedy, came to me. I, however, having examined the breast—which was completely inflamed, twice as large as the other, and with a quantity of thinner humors <note>In ancient and medieval medicine, "humors" were the bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) whose balance determined health. An "affluence of thin humors" suggests a watery, unhealthy discharge from the wound.</note> flowing to the wound—wished to try everything before I would allow the poor woman to perish. I focused my mind on this **balsam**, wanting to test whether that which had fulfilled the promises of its Author in other diseases would fail here.

And so, as the sick woman waited for my answer with an eager face <note>original: "ore hiante"; literally "with a gaping mouth," signifying her desperate anxiety and hope for a cure.</note>, I told her that within eight days I would pronounce whether any hope of health might shine forth without the remedy of surgical removal. At the same time, I gave her this remedy so that she might anoint her breast with it. It was a wonderful thing: within two days the matter reached concoction <note>original: "concoctionem"; the medical process where the body's heat "ripened" or processed morbid humors into a state where they could be healed or expelled.</note> and attained a proper consistency. Full of good hope, I then added internal and external remedies which seemed appropriate for the condition, and within the space of two months, the woman attained full health. I, for my part, not without a peculiar joy, blessed the Lord, who bestowed such great virtue upon this balsam.

# Basilius Valentinus

<meta>catchword: In-</meta>

I have sought this for many years, and at last, with God as my guide, I found it. Do you wish for me to speak of it to you through a riddle? I see you are eager; receive this great mystery. "*Make haste slowly*," <note>original: *Festina lente*</note> as Alciatus <note>Andrea Alciati (1492–1550), an Italian jurist and author of a famous book of "emblems" or allegorical illustrations.</note> depicts, where he winds a dolphin around an anchor. Consider this to be especially useful to you in this matter, as in all of life; for as you know, *the hasty bitch brings forth blind pups*: and so I warn you again and again, that through Juno, Bacchus, and Vulcan <note>Alchemical allegories for the elements or stages of heating: Juno often represents Air, Bacchus represents the "spirit" or alcohol, and Vulcan represents Fire.</note> you must take care to prepare its wings, but by your soul, do not permit it to fly suddenly. Give it to Mercury to be educated, so that it may gradually become accustomed to flight; indeed, keep it bound with a rope, lest like a bird released from its cage and set beyond your power, it should approach too near the sun through ignorance, and like Icarus, fall headlong into the sea with scorched feathers. But later, in its own time, you shall slacken the reins so that it may fly forth and reach those Blessed Isles <note>The Fortunate Isles: a legendary alchemical metaphor for the successful completion of the "Great Work."</note> toward which all sons of the art <term>Sons of the Art: a traditional term for alchemists who have been initiated into the secrets of the craft</term> direct their eyes, and where all who have obtained the desired and long-sought end have arrived.

At this point you distort your face and wrinkle your brow, Philomathes <note>A Greek-derived name meaning "lover of learning," often used in alchemical dialogues to represent the student or seeker.</note>, and are you indignant, as if I had mocked you while you stood gaping by showing you the apple of Tantalus <note>A reference to the Greek myth of Tantalus, who was punished with eternal hunger and thirst, with food and water always receding when he reached for them.</note>? What should I do? I consult you yourself: do you wish me to cast pearls before swine here, and expose the mystery to everyone—the mystery which our ancestors kept so sacredly to be revealed only to the sons of the art worthy of such great privilege? You yourself would grieve most of all and would curse me if I did so.

<summary>Basilius Valentinus uses classical mythology and emblems to caution the reader that the secret of antimony requires extreme patience and slow, controlled heating. He defends his use of riddles, arguing that the "Great Work" must remain hidden from the unworthy.</summary>

### Of Basil Valentine

...to a marvel, it expels the root of the disease and restores whatever has been corrupted by any symptom.

But common <term>glass of antimony <note>Latin: vitrum antimonii. A glass-like substance created by melting antimony ore; it was a notoriously violent emetic if not prepared with extreme care.</note></term> is finely ground, then six grains or more (depending on the strength of the patient’s constitution) are placed in a little wine in a warm place overnight. In the morning, leaving the powder behind, the wine is [poured off]...

<margin>* And this is the common vomitory, or **emetic wine**, which nowadays all the <term>traveling quacks <note>Latin: circumforanei. Literally "those who frequent the markets"; unlicensed, wandering doctors who sold miracle cures to the public.</note></term> use everywhere—sometimes with a successful, sometimes with an opposite, but always with a doubtful outcome. This result stems partly from the Physician and partly from the medicine itself. As histories relate, **George Castriot**, King of the Epirotes <note>Better known as Scanderbeg (1405–1468), the celebrated Albanian commander who led a rebellion against the Ottoman Empire.</note>, had sent his sword—the performer of so many wonders—to **Mehmed**, the Emperor of the Turks, who had repeatedly demanded it. When the Turk returned it according to their treaty, he remarked that he saw nothing remarkable in the blade. Castriot replied that he had indeed sent Scanderbeg’s sword, but not Scanderbeg’s arm, which gave life to that sword to perform such great miracles. So it is with most Chemical Medicines, especially either [deadly]...

# The Triumph of Antimony. 169

...of your [health], or, what is even more deplorable, are often the causes of death, when they are not directed by the hand of a skilled Doctor who knows how to administer them at the proper time and place. The cause of a failed outcome that arises from the medicine itself is that it remains too heavily impregnated with "mercurial" properties—as Basil <note>Referring to Basil Valentine, the legendary alchemist and supposed author of this text.</note> rightly notes here—which I am accustomed to correcting in the following manner:

*Take four ounces of the purest glass of antimony <term>Glass of antimony: A transparent, vitreous substance produced by roasting stibnite and melting the resulting ash; used here as a medicinal base.</term>; half an ounce of Venetian borax <note>Borax was used as a flux to help the minerals melt and purify.</note>; melt them together. When this is done skillfully, you will obtain a green and transparent glass like an Emerald. Grind this finely and pour French wine over it; let it rest in a warm place for several days. Of this wine, give the patient from one to two ounces, according to their age, physical constitution, strength, and other factors—whether natural or non-natural <term>Non-naturals: In early medicine, these were six external factors that influenced health, including air, diet, sleep, and exercise.</term>—which the prudence of a Physician always takes into counsel while sitting as the arbiter of someone's life or death.*

Experience—the most certain teacher of physicians—has taught me that this emetic wine can be offered to the sick without the danger that usually stems from the medicine itself. Furthermore, when the wine poured over the powder has been used up, if you pour on fresh wine, it will be imbued with the same medicinal powers. For here antimony reveals that which is the most admirable of all its wonders: that it contains within itself inexhaustible treasures; although you take from it, you do not diminish its strength. This is...

I poured off the tincture gently, and having replaced it in a retort, I softly drew off the moisture so that a reddish powder remained at the bottom. ### Of Basil Valentine ###

...they stimulate the matter, strengthen the powers, and—so that I do not overwhelm the intelligent reader with words—in all diseases, both of the body and others, it is a **universal medicine** <note>original: "medicina universalis"; a mythical substance capable of curing all illness and prolonging life</note>.

Since mention has been made here of the most excellent of all **tinctures** <term>tinctura: an alchemical extract or medicinal essence thought to contain the "soul" of a substance</term>, I will teach the **Chemist** <note>original: "Chymicum"</note> once and for all something that will be of use to him everywhere. Namely, that it matters greatly by which **solvent** <term>menstruo: a liquid used to dissolve or extract the essence from a solid</term> each tincture is extracted. For it is not enough—which is the mistaken opinion of many—that they simply lack corrosiveness and are "sweet" solvents; rather, it is necessary that they have a peculiar affinity and compatibility with the **mercury of the body** <note>mercurio corporis: in alchemical theory, the principle of fluidity and connectivity within a physical substance</note> over which they are poured, so that they may extract the true and pure **sulfur** <note>sulphur: the alchemical principle of "soul," color, and combustibility</note> from it. 

I will teach you more clearly what I mean by an example. **Distilled water** extracts a tincture from the sulfur that has been produced through distilled vinegar from **glass of antimony** <note>vitro antimonii: a translucent, reddish-brown glass created by melting roasted antimony ore</note>. But if you should persuade yourself that the pure has now been separated from the impure, you are mistaken; for that water absorbs a certain **salt** which contaminates the tincture. However, **rectified spirit of wine** <note>spiritus vini rectificatus: high-proof distilled ethanol</note> is its proper solvent, for this takes up only its volatile sulfur and has no dealings with the salt.

The same thing happens in the **tincture of corals**, which is extracted with spirit of wine distilled over orange peels; for here you will not obtain a pure tincture of corals, because through this solvent the pure sulfur from the body of the coral—
