-># The Triumph of Antimony<-

...of the corals is not separated; and experience has revealed this error to me in many instances—experience which would be the best teacher, if it did not demand a tuition fee <term>didactrum|A Greek-derived term for a teacher's fee or the cost of instruction</term> in this art that is often far too precious.

But to conclude regarding the tincture, from which this entire discussion has flowed: let no one think that this is the "Potable Gold" <term>aurum potabile|Literally "drinkable gold," a legendary alchemical cordial believed to be a universal cure-all</term> of the Philosophers. For such a belief would be the greatest error in Philosophy, and would give an opportunity to the Sophists <note>In this context, "Sophists" refers to fraudulent alchemists who used trickery to mimic chemical transformations for profit</note> to peddle their wares beyond the truth, as they are accustomed to do. For although this is a most precious tincture, and indeed a "tincture of gold," it has only assumed the color of gold; it has not inherited the weight of gold, which is the defining characteristic of true Potable Gold.

This is not the place to treat that potable medicine, lest I overstep the boundaries I have set for myself in commenting upon the *Triumphal Chariot of Antimony*, which our Basil Valentine <note>Basilius Valentinus, a legendary 15th-century alchemist and monk whose works focused heavily on the medicinal uses of antimony</note> constructed as a truly magnificent work. On another occasion, I would have to explain how the Sun <note>original: "sol"; the alchemical symbol and name for Gold</note> can be prepared through Venus <note>Copper</note> and Vulcan <note>Fire</note>, so that in the space of two hours it resolves itself into a mercury united with Bacchus <note>Wine or spirit of wine (alcohol)</note>, leaving behind only small dregs. This mercurial solvent <term>menstruum|An alchemical term for a solvent capable of dissolving a solid body</term> is then separated again from the dissolved gold; in this way, you acquire a liquid of the heaviest weight, which can never thereafter be returned to its former solid consistency.

I have demonstrated this operation to certain curious "friends of chemistry" <term>philochymici|From the Greek, meaning those who love or study the art of chemistry/alchemy</term>. However, another time and place will be granted to discuss these matters further.

### Basilius Valentinus

*...has come forth through distillation, pour on common distilled rain water, which has been heated beforehand, and remove all salt by washing <note>original: dulcorando; literally "sweetening," the alchemical process of washing a precipitate to remove corrosive salts until the runoff is tasteless</note>, so that no sharpness remains. ->then<-

Those who think they will achieve wonders by merely mixing <note>original: "confundendo"; literally "pouring together" or "confusing," referring to a physical mixture rather than a chemical reaction</note> two things together know best how important this union is in Chemistry <term>Chymia: the early modern study of chemical substances, encompassing both alchemy and early chemistry</term>. Separate these, but the secret essence <term>arcanum: a hidden, powerful substance or "secret" of nature</term> remains mixed with the spirit of wine and of vitriol <note>sulfuric acid</note>: when

...[it] was not present before; but by union, a certain spirit is generated in things—I know not what—which performs those feats that would never have entered a man's mind as being able to emerge from there. Notice here in the generation of animals (who would believe it!) that from the union of elements, sight, taste, touch, and so many animal powers can be generated—powers which reside in none of the individual elements, and yet emerge from them once united. If it ever happens that some tincture <term>tincture: a concentrated medicinal or alchemical extract, often carrying the "soul" or essential properties of a metal</term> seems to have united itself with a solvent <term>menstruum: a liquid used to dissolve or extract solids</term> through digestion <note>prolonged gentle heating, often at the temperature of a nesting bird or dung heap</note>, and is afterwards separated, this occurs because the marriage was not legitimately celebrated, nor was the union perfected in the proper manner. This is something you will see happen more than once in the tincture of Gold <note>original: "solis" (the Sun), the alchemical name for Gold</note> and of Antimony.

The instrument with which I am accustomed to join my tinctures and couple them into an inseparable society, you will find depicted later in this very book. This must be noted regarding the spirit of wine—which does not happen so in other solvents—that it is most easily united with other things, and yet is separated from them again by a slight artifice <note>a simple technique or chemical process</note>.

Here, perhaps some Physician will wonder how it can be that this medicine produces softer stools, since **antimony** <note>A brittle, silvery-gray metalloid. <margin>* Note here again and again that Galenic cathartics <term>Galenica: Traditional herbal medicines based on the theories of the Greek physician Galen, which focused on balancing the four humors</term> indeed have the power to draw out humors, but they do not have the power to correct them. Chemical remedies <term>Chymica: Early modern medicines produced through laboratory processes like distillation and sublimation, often called iatrochemistry</term>, however, are powerful in both capacities. Certainly, it is not how much "filth" is drawn out of the body that matters, but how much of the body is actually healed. This is achieved more through the hidden <note>original: "occulta"</note> power of chemically prepared medicines than by the public violence of purging.</margin>

# Basil Valentine

->The Triumphal Chariot of Antimony. 190<-

[it] heats from within; yet if it is applied externally, it draws all the heat of an inflamed part to itself. On the other hand, when <term>vinegar<note>original: "acetum"</note></term> has been made from wine, it cools whether applied internally or externally, even though both the <term>spirit<note>In this context, distilled alcohol.</note></term> and the vinegar come from the same lineage and have arisen from the same root.

The reason for this difference is that vinegar is produced by <term>digestion<note>original: "digestionem." In alchemy, this refers to the process of exposing a substance to gentle, consistent heat, mimicking biological digestion to transform its properties.</note></term> alone, which is followed by the <term>putrefaction<note>The stage of decomposition or fermentation through which a substance was thought to "die" before being reborn in a purified state.</note></term> of the wine, along with a vegetable <term>fixation<note>original: "fixitatione." The process by which a volatile substance is made stable and resistant to heat.</note></term>. In contrast, the spirit of wine is produced by separation through distillation, or vegetable <term>sublimation<note>The process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then condensing it again to purify it.</note></term>, which renders the spirit <term>volatile<note>original: "volatilem." A substance that evaporates easily; the opposite of "fixed."</note></term>. By a similar reasoning, <term>antimony<note>A metallic element that was a cornerstone of "iatrochemistry" (chemical medicine). Valentine argues its medicinal value depends entirely on how it is chemically processed.</note></term> is prepared; and according to these diverse preparations, it produces diverse effects, and communicates its gifts to us in various ways,

<summary>Valentine explains how the same starting material, such as wine, can produce either a heating spirit or a cooling vinegar depending on the chemical process used—digestion versus distillation. # The Triumphal Chariot of Antimony

...which are scarcely comprehensible to the human intellect. However, its **fixity** <note>In alchemy, 'fixity' refers to a substance's ability to withstand the heat of fire without evaporating or changing.</note>, which I am discussing here, is prepared in this manner:

<margin>Fixed powder of antimony.</margin>
Take as much **antimony** as you wish, grind it finely, and place the powder into a **cucurbit** <term>a gourd-shaped glass vessel used as the base of a still</term>. Pour on enough **strong water** <term>original: "aqua fortis," usually referring to nitric acid</term> so that it stands at a depth of six fingers. Let it be placed in gentle heat for ten days, well **luted** <note>sealed with a specialized paste or clay to make the vessel airtight</note> and closed, so that the essence may be extracted. Pour off this pure and clear extraction by **decantation** <note>the process of pouring off the top layer of liquid without disturbing the sediment at the bottom</note>, and filter it so that it is freed from all dregs and impurities. 

Then, place the extraction into a glass vessel and remove all the strong water through **distillation** in ashes or sand <note>methods of providing indirect, even heat to the glass vessel</note>. A yellow and dry powder of antimony will remain at the bottom. Pour distilled rainwater onto this, and let it be placed in a similar glass vessel in moderate heat; you will then obtain a **red extraction**. Filter this again, and by a **water-** <meta>The catchword "bal-" indicates "balneum Mariae," or a water bath, which will be described on the following page.</meta>

<summary>This page describes the laboratory process for creating a "fixed" powder of antimony using nitric acid, digestion, and distillation, resulting in a yellow powder and a red extract.</summary>

...distill the rainwater gently in a **water bath** <term>original: "balneum mariæ," a vessel of water in which another container is placed to heat slowly and evenly</term>, and a **red powder** will remain. ### The Triumph of Antimony.
I

<margin>The powers of the fixed powder.</margin>
...[se]cond, which works in a wondrous manner. Taken in a dose of half a <term>drachm<note>An apothecary weight equivalent to approximately 3.9 grams or 1/8th of an ounce.</note></term> three times a day—namely in the morning, at noon, and in the evening, or even more frequently—for it does not harm a person. It radically cures the <term>French disease<note>original: "morbum Gallicum"; a common early modern name for syphilis.</note></term>, causes new hair to grow, and remarkably renews the whole person.

Since I have now taught how to prepare the fixed powder of antimony, as well as an extraction entirely suitable for use, I shall now leave that subject behind. For the <term>Philosopher<note>In alchemical literature, the "Philosopher" refers to a true practitioner of the Hermetic art who understands the deep secrets of nature.</note></term> must apply himself with the utmost accuracy while preparing this powder. It would be a grave matter if these mysteries of such great effects were handled by common hands, or if they were made for sale at a price. Let him who has obtained this little fixed powder use it for <term>chronic diseases</term>, especially those where sweats must be induced, and he will see results that will bring him joy if he uses it on himself, and glory if he uses it on others.

# Basilius Valentinus
## Page 194

...discourse, I have proposed to treat briefly of the **flowers of antimony**<note>In alchemy, "flowers" refer to the fine powder produced through sublimation—the process of heating a substance until it becomes a vapor and then cooling it back into a solid form.</note>, which can be prepared in many ways. Regarding these, indeed * the greatest part

***

* To what extent **Chemistry** [Alchemy] was attacked by slanders during the time of Basilius is evident from the many surviving writings. Certain **Theologians**, passing imprudent judgment on things they did not understand, and **Politicians**, not much wiser than they, defamed their own books with such attacks. In doing so, they gave those who have examined the matter more deeply reason to believe that these critics thundered their other judgments against the innocent with just as little caution. I am not referring here to those who exposed certain traveling <term>Sophists<note>In this context, "Sophists" refers to fraudulent alchemists or "charlatans" who used the prestige of the art to deceive people for money.</note></term> who hid their crimes under the pretext of this most noble art and forced great frauds upon the public using a famous name. Indeed, this class of men deserves not only censure but even physical punishment. 

But what, I ask, have the good [practitioners] deserved if evil men abuse their name? Why is an art so certain, so healing, and so useful outlawed simply because men are found who abuse not the art itself, but only its name and shadow? But I do not wish to spin this <term>apology<note>original: "apologia"; a formal written defense of a belief or field of study.</note></term> any further, lest by chance <term>envy<note>original: "invidia"; the author suggests that jealousy of the alchemists' secrets is the true root of the public's hostility.</note></term>—which was the primary cause of these slanders—falls upon me while I am thus lamenting, and

# The Triumph of Antimony. 195

When I hear Basilius <note>Basil Valentine, the legendary 15th-century alchemist and monk whose works were championed by the author</note> arming his followers, I have reason to applaud our times, in which that very dense cloud—which used to hurl such great thunder—has now somewhat faded away. In the apothecaries' shops, medicines are now displayed that are, as they say, chemically prepared. Even those very men who most wish to be called Hippocratic <note>followers of traditional medicine based on the ancient Greek physician Hippocrates, who often opposed the new "chemical" doctors</note> hardly dare to condemn Chemistry <note>original: Chymia</note>, and they frequently bring back into use things that they cannot deny have proceeded from the chemical art. 

Are those times finally approaching in which **Elias the Artist** <note>Helias Artista: a mythical figure in alchemical prophecy, popularized by Paracelsus, who was expected to appear at the end of the world to reveal the ultimate secrets of nature and alchemy</note> will arrive—the revealer of the greater mysteries? Paracelsus <term>prophetically</term> <note>original Greek: μαντιῶδες (mantiōdes)</note> predicts his coming so clearly in various places. Perhaps it will be worthwhile, for the consolation of the oppressed disciples of Basilius, to cite several passages from Paracelsus in which he predicts the coming of Elias (who was not yet born at that time). If one interprets these passages properly—as the other sayings of that man should be taken—one will find nothing absurd in them, unless one wishes to display one's own absurd stupidity or wicked envy. In his book *On Minerals*, chapter 8, he writes as follows:

> *That which is of less value, God allows to be revealed; however, that which is of greater importance still lies hidden from the common people until the coming of **Elias the Artist**—or as others read it, until the art of Elias—when he shall come.*

And again in the *Book of Minerals*, first treatise:

> *It is indeed true, he says, that many things still lie hidden and concealed in the earth, which I, along with others, am ignorant of. For I know this: that God will thereafter reveal many wonders—*

# Of Basilius Valentinus

[Ba]bylonian. If any of the fools should hear this discourse of mine, he will say that I am either insane or soaked in wine; <note>original: "vino madere." This is a biblical allusion to Acts 2:13, where the Apostles were accused of being drunk when they spoke through the Holy Spirit.</note> but this will be a slander shared by me with the disciples of our Savior. ### The Triumph of Antimony

...they cause more harm than good. Thus, this process seems to me to have been written so obscurely by that religious man <note>likely referring to the author, Basil Valentine, often depicted as a Benedictine monk</note> (for why would he not want to reveal the matter?) only so that the "sons of the art" <note>a traditional term for initiated alchemists</note> might understand that he himself was not ignorant of it. However, so that you, the reader, may understand that you have not spent your money in buying nor your time in reading these commentaries in vain, I shall clearly and plainly set forth to you how I have often completed it: pay attention to every detail, so that you do not fail anywhere; for the operations are linked together in such a way that if one is omitted or performed carelessly, the others will collapse, and your labor will not meet your desires.

*Take the best <term>antimony ore<note>original: "antimonii minera"; the raw mineral stibnite as found in the earth, rather than the commercially smelted metal.</note></term>, which should be brittle (for if you take crude antimony, as Basil appears to suggest, you will achieve nothing, because it has already lost its most subtle <term>sulfur<note>In alchemy, "sulfur" refers to the combustible, soul-like principle of a substance, not just the chemical element S.</note></term> during its first distillation). Grind it so finely that it passes through a fine linen sieve; place it in a <term>cucurbit<note>A gourd-shaped glass vessel used as the lower part of a distillation apparatus.</note></term>, and pour over it vinegar which has been distilled according to the art from its own ore. Place it in digestion for 40 days, and if you have accurately observed everything required here, your vinegar will be tinged with a red color like blood. This red <term>tincture<note>A concentrated medicinal or alchemical extract.</note></term>, poured off by tilting the vessel, place in a <term>retort<note>A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids.</note></term>, and gently draw off the vinegar. The powder that remains, extract with <term>spirit of wine<note>original: "spiritu vini"; high-percentage alcohol or ethanol.</note></term>, so that it again takes on a blood-red color. Put that extraction into a <term>circulatory vessel<note>original: "circulatorio"; a sealed vessel designed so that vapors rise, condense, and flow back into the liquid, allowing for continuous reflux.</note></term> similar to the one I have arranged to be depicted here, because no one ever—*

### The Triumph of Antimony

...by leaching the material. There shall be made \* the **liver**, as has been done many times before.

\* While the liver is a subject of much talk at banquets, the **liver of antimony** <term>hepar antimonii<gloss>A reddish-brown compound of sulfur and antimony, named for its liver-like color</gloss></term> will be of no small importance to us in chemical operations. And first of all, take note that it is better to use **salt of tartar** <note>original: salem tartari; this is potassium carbonate, used as a flux</note> in place of raw tartar. This is not only to shorten the operation (for otherwise the mixture must be melted for a long time until the tartar is naturally converted into salt), but especially so that the **glass** <term>vitrum<gloss>a translucent, solid substance produced by melting metallic ores with a flux</gloss></term> may take on a far <margin>.6</margin> better color. **Basil Valentine** <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemist, the purported author of 'The Triumphant Chariot of Antimony'</note> also forgets—or perhaps for certain reasons chooses to omit—to warn that the glass must be poured into a previously heated basin and crushed in a previously heated mortar. Otherwise, because of the salt of tartar, this glass absorbs moisture from the air so quickly that it is immediately turned into a muddy sludge or pulp. Furthermore, he fails to specify the quantity of water in which the ground powder should be dissolved to obtain a more beautiful and saturated color; it is in this intensification of color that the primary strength of the substance resides. He also remains silent regarding the **precipitation** of the "liver" from the water, which must be performed using **vinegar** <note>original: aceto</note>. Yet these steps, and those already mentioned, are absolutely necessary in this operation.

Nor shall I neglect to warn of this: that both in this and in every **fusion of antimony**...

I do not wish to digress into a proof of these virtues of celestial influences against men who are so little learned that they do not understand, or so inexperienced that they have not seen them—and who are, meanwhile, so stubborn that they do not believe those who *have* seen, *have* experienced, and confirm that the matter is so. Those men, I—along with those who have so often not just seen it done with their eyes, but performed it with their own hands—consider more worthy of contempt than of refutation. For who can overturn that boorish *I deny*, *I deny*? <note>original: nego, nego; a common Latin phrase used to describe those who refuse to accept evidence regardless of proof</note> These stubborn men sometimes irritate the patience of good men and those initiated into the art with that way of acting, thinking that the adepts will go so far, out of a certain zeal to confirm what they affirm, as to show them a demonstration of this noble art. But I do not think anyone who has attained the **mystery** <note>original: mysterium; referring to the secret knowledge of the Great Work</note> would be so imprudent as to rashly **cast pearls before swine**, and...

# The Triumph of Antimony

...[so that] fire may show what God has revealed to him, and of what bread he eats, which is not to be cast to the dogs <note>This is a biblical allusion to Matthew 15:26, suggesting that sacred alchemical knowledge should not be shared with the unworthy.</note>.

Returning then to the matter from which I digressed, I affirm that there are certain times of the year in which, if the <term>liver of antimony</term> <note>original: *hepar antimonii*; a reddish-brown substance created by fusing antimony ore with salts</note> is made and precipitated with the proper <term>solvent</term> <note>original: *menstruo*; in alchemy, a liquid used to dissolve or extract substances</note>, it is imbued with an entirely different color and possesses different powers than that which is prepared at another time. This applies both to the transformation of metals and to the healing of men, as I myself have experienced in many serious illnesses and symptoms of disease.

I will also add this: from the liver of antimony, a <term>redness</term> <note>original: *rubedinem*; a red essence or tincture believed to hold the medicinal "soul" of the mineral</note> can be extracted using <term>spirit of wine</term> <note>original: *spiritum vini*; concentrated alcohol or ethanol</note>, which is volatile and distilled through an <term>alembic</term> <note>an early chemical still consisting of two vessels connected by a tube</note>. This can be administered to everyone without danger. Nor is there much need for that caution of <note>Basil Valentine, the legendary 15th-century monk and alchemist to whom this work is attributed</note> Basil, even before the redness becomes volatile, suggesting it should be administered only in small measures.

For when given in doses of 30, 40, or 50 drops, it excites very little sweat, much less does it move the bowels or cause vomiting. Instead, it performs those wonders of purifying the blood, uprooting the causes of disease, and restoring a person to vigor through <term>insensible perspiration</term> <note>original: *insensibilem expirationem*; the release of toxins through the skin or breath without visible sweating</note> and its own hidden powers. This "wine" is so good it needs no ivy-garland <note>original: *eloquentiæ hederâ*; an allusion to the proverb "Good wine needs no bush." In the past, an ivy bush or garland was hung outside a tavern to show wine was for sale. The author means the remedy is so effective it requires no fancy advertising</note> to recommend it to the sick; those who have once experienced its powers know its worth, how fervently they should seek it, and at what great price they should value it.

### Of Basil Valentine

Instead, one floats upon the other like water and oil, which cannot be mixed. <margin>A miracle of nature.</margin> But if that same extracted spirit of wine is poured over another liver of antimony, it will again draw the essence to itself; yet, even when this is poured over the first extraction, it still will not mix. This phenomenon is counted among the wonders...

...lest your vessels be broken. \* In order to sharpen the diligence of readers, and even more so to ensure that no one except true students of the art might penetrate their inner sanctuaries <note>original: "adyta," referring to the innermost, most sacred part of a temple</note>, Chemists do not hand down everything in a single place. Instead, they scatter their instructions so that through diligent collection—much like the eagle is said to test its legitimate offspring by their ability to gaze directly into the sun—they may prove who are the true sons of the art. Thus you see Basilius <note>Basilius Valentinus, the legendary 15th-century alchemist and monk to whom this work is attributed</note> here proposing the use of "water of mercury," the preparation of which he taught in the treatise called the *Supplement*—or rather, he sketched its process there with a "rough coal" <note>a metaphor meaning he provided only a crude or veiled outline</note>. For he did not mention there, as he finally does here, the hole which ought to stand open at the top of the retort <note>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distillation</note>, so that a tube may be inserted into it, through which the mercury may be injected in small portions. For if you were to enclose even the slightest bit of mercury in the retort and drive it with a vehement fire—as must be done to extract its spirit—it would overturn the retort and the furnace itself with its violent and indomitable force, unless you give it a wider space and greater freedom to fly; so that at length, after it has visited many retorts, it may rest and settle down as if exhausted. Over a period of three months, act so that they no longer flee from <term>Vulcan: the alchemical fire</term>, but rather play most peacefully with and within the flames. If you achieve this, you have two substances joined in an inseparable marriage: you have separated the pure from the impure, you have rendered the volatile fixed and the fixed volatile, and you are but a short distance from that ultimate happiness <note>a reference to the successful completion of the alchemical Great Work</note> which you have sought with all your heart.

### Of Basil Valentine

226

*White powder of antimony.*

Take pure <term>antimony: a brittle, silvery-white mineral used in alchemical medicine</term> that is brought from Hungary, or found in similar mountainous regions; grind it finely. Take also an equal measure of the purest <term>saltpeter: also known as niter (potassium nitrate)</term>, which has been purified three times with all diligence. This composition is burned together in a new glazed pot that has never been tainted by any grease, using a <term>circular fire: a heat source that surrounds the vessel to ensure even temperature</term>. Do not add the mixture all at once, but rather add it in portions at different times. The old <term>Spagyrics: alchemists following the tradition of Paracelsus, focused on "separating and recombining" elements</term> call this method of acting "to detonate" <note>original: "detonare"</note>, a technical term that a student of the art must learn, as it is not a term known to common laborers in their own experience or craft. Once this operation is completed, finely grind the hard matter that remained in the pot. In a glazed pot, pour common water over it, heated to a moderate degree; once the matter has settled, repeat this pouring several times until it extracts <note>The Latin text ends with "extraha," which matches the catchword "traha" from the previous page's metadata.</note> all the saltpeter,

# Of Basil Valentine <note>Basilius Valentinus, the legendary 15th-century alchemist and monk to whom this work is attributed</note>

*...add as much as its weight is [of saltpeter]; burn it off again, and repeat the same operation a third time: finally, grind it.*

Take 8, 9, 10, or 11 grains of it, according to the patient’s strength and other indicating factors; pour over these three or four ounces of wine. For it matters little whether you take more or less wine. Place it in a water bath <term>water bath|*balneum*, a vessel of hot water used to heat another container gently and evenly</term> for a period of four or five hours. Extract the tincture of the **saffron of metals** <term>saffron of metals|*crocus metallorum*, a reddish-brown antimony compound</term>, which is red like blood (an effect that does not occur in the infusion of **common saffron** <term>common saffron|*crocus officinalis*, the botanical spice</term>). This wine, now saturated with the sulfur <note>original: *sulphure*; in alchemy, "sulfur" often refers to the oily, combustible, or "soul" principle of a substance that provides its medicinal properties</note> of the saffron, together with the finer part of the powder which passes through during decanting, I administer to the sick person. They are purged gently, internally, and both from above and below <note>original: *internè, & supernè*; referring to the dual action of emetics (inducing vomiting) and laxatives (inducing bowel movements), which were standard goals in early modern medicine</note> without distress. This medicine does not only draw out the humors <term>humors|the bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) whose balance was thought to determine health</term>, but as is characteristic of properly prepared antimonials, it attacks the root of the disease. If anything in the body is corrupt or weakened, it cleanses and restores it.

I will refrain from listing what wonderful effects this single tincture has shown me, lest I be forced to call upon the testimony of those who have experienced them. Believe me at least in this, whoever you are who restores chemical medicines <term>chemical medicines|*chymica medicamenta*, early pharmaceutical preparations derived from minerals and metals through alchemical processes</term> to use: always obtain the true tinctures of things, in which their volatile sulfur is hidden. If you neglect this, you neglect your own reputation and profit, and the health of your patient.

# Basilius Valentinus

...of the whitest and best-baked bread to eat, which he did not prepare by his own labor, or for which he himself did not thresh the grain with his own flail <note>original: *tribulo*; a threshing sledge used to separate grain from husks</note>.

But I venture too far into this open field of doctrine, in which the hunters of old catch their larks, and the young men immediately follow them with their nets. For my style (as all my writings more than adequately testify) has a certain unique manner set before it, just as was the case with all the Philosophers <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers specifically to alchemists and seekers of the "Great Work"</note> before me. If anyone should wonder, however, that certain processes are proposed here, in which Phi-

<column-break/>

> —losophical offspring. It is not right that the son of the lowliest shepherd <note>original: *opilionis*</note> should eat of the finest flour <note>original: *polline*; refers to the finest, most refined part of the grain</note> which cost him no sweat in the threshing <note>original: *triturando*</note>. ->The Triumph of Antimony. 233<-

My philosophy differs from others: let this be my response, that philosophical discourse differs greatly from the manner of discourse used by others, who declare a process plainly and purely without any circumlocutions or riddles. Note the difference, then, and do not accuse me as if I had strayed from the proper order regarding the style of philosophy, preparations, and processes. In philosophical discourse, one must learn and judge what pertains to theory, but the instruction of processes is taught to you by <term>praxis|the practical, hands-on application of theory</term>; therefore, in those matters, one must use words that are true, simple, clear, and well-founded. <note>The author is defending his use of plain language for the actual recipes, contrasting it with the more veiled or "enigmatic" language common in alchemical philosophy.</note>

A balsam is also made from <term>antimony|a metallic mineral used in both medicine and metallurgy</term> which is very useful for serious illnesses. It is not made from the raw ore, however, but from its <term>regulus|the purified metallic "heart" of the ore</term>, from which the living mercury of antimony can be prepared in the manner that follows.

# Basil Valentine #

<margin>Regulus of antimony.</margin> Take equal parts of the best Hungarian <term>antimony|a metallic mineral used in both medicine and metallurgy</term> and crude <term>tartar|a potassium salt (potassium bitartrate) found in wine casks</term>, and a half part of <term>saltpeter|potassium nitrate, used here as a flux to help the minerals melt</term>. Let them be ground together finely, and after they have melted together in a <term>wind furnace|a furnace that uses a draft of air to reach high temperatures</term>, let them be poured into a bronze basin, and let them cool there; then the <term>regulus|the pure metallic "button" that settles at the bottom of the crucible</term> is found— <note>original: "regulus"</note>

* What I noted in the preparation of the <term>liver of antimony|a brownish-red, opaque mass of sulfurated antimony</term> must be repeated here: that you should take <term>salt of tartar|potassium carbonate, a more refined and reactive form than crude tartar</term> instead of crude tartar, so that the operation proceeds more quickly and safely. Saltpeter here is useless. Therefore, take equal parts of antimony and salt of tartar; from this, melt the regulus according to the rule handed down here by Basil. If you throw away the <term>glass|the slag or vitrified byproduct of the smelting process</term> (as nearly everyone does), you do so to no purpose. For from that glass I prepare a medicine that is useful above all others, in this way: I crush this glass in a heated mortar, taking care that it absorbs no moisture from the air (which can be prevented easily enough). I place this in a vial, from which <term>alcohol of wine|highly distilled wine spirit or ethanol</term> extracts a most beautiful tincture of a red color, like blood. This tincture is a most excellent <term>cordial|a restorative medicine meant to invigorate the heart and spirit</term> if 30, 40, or 50 drops are taken in a suitable liquid; and this may be done, if you wish, two or three times a day: for it is taken safely, and it completely re- <note>The text cuts off here, continuing with the catchword "restores" or "recreates."</note>

### The Triumph of Antimony

[regu]lus; this is purified by fire three times, or even more often, with tartar <term>tartar: potassium bitartrate, a sediment from wine casks used as a flux</term> and saltpeter <term>salt nitre: potassium nitrate, used here to help separate the metal from impurities</term>, and it becomes white and shining like cupelled silver <term>cupellation: a refining process where metal is heated in a porous bowl to remove base metals like lead</term>, which has already flashed <note>original: "fulminavit"; in metallurgy, this refers to the 'blick' or the sudden brightening of silver when it reaches purity</note> and has overcome all its lead.

...in fire like a **Salamander** <note>In alchemy, the salamander was a legendary creature believed to live in and consume fire.</note>: and furthermore, many wonderful animals are found in hotter regions and islands that are unknown to these nations; these creatures prolong their lives by the heat of the **Sun** alone, and die as soon as they encounter a different atmosphere. Finally, place everything into a <term>cucurbit</term> <note>original: "cucurbitæ"; a gourd-shaped glass vessel used for distillation</note>, and in an ash-bath distill the vinegar so that the salts remain dry.

* Those things which in the time of **Basil** <note>Referring to the author, Basil Valentine</note> were the highest **secrets** <note>original: "arcana"; hidden mysteries of nature</note> are in our own times already common works of the chemists. For who is there, among those who call themselves true chemists, who does not know the method for producing mercury from antimony—either by this way which Basil teaches, or by another? ### The Triumph of Antimony

[mercury] through leather, pour on four parts of highly rectified red oil of vitriol <term>oil of vitriol: a historical term for concentrated sulfuric acid, called "red" when it contains certain impurities or is highly concentrated</term>, draw off the oil, and the spirits of the oil will remain with the mercury. Drive it off with a strong fire, and something will be sublimated <term>sublimation: the chemical process where a solid turns into a gas and then back into a solid without becoming a liquid</term>. Place this sublimate back onto the sediment <note>original: *terrae in fundo*, literally "the earth at the bottom"</note> at the bottom. Pour on again another portion of oil of the same weight as before; repeat this a third time. On the fourth occasion, however, take the sublimate that has risen along with the sediment and grind them together; then it will be clear and pure like a mirror or crystal. Place it in a circulatory vessel <term>circulatory: a glass alchemical vessel where vapors are condensed and returned to the base, allowing for constant reflux</term>, and pour on as much oil of vitriol and three times as much spirit of wine <note>highly concentrated ethanol or alcohol</note>. Let it circulate until a separation occurs, and eventually, the mercury will resolve itself into an oil and will float on top like olive oil. When this has been achieved, separate that oil from the others and place it in a glass circulatory vessel; pour on strong distilled vinegar and allow it to rest in this manner for approximately [twenty] days

* There is no need for torches at high noon, nor for any commentary on such a clear description, by which Basil <note>Referring to Basilius Valentinus, the legendary 15th-century author and alchemist</note> teaches how to prepare the oil of mercury from antimony <term>antimony: a metallic element that alchemists believed held the "spirit" necessary for transforming base metals</term>. Proceed step-by-step, pay attention to every detail, and your labor will never fail you. Lest I spread darkness, I shall abstain from a commentary; however, I add that I do not doubt this mercury performs wonderful effects in the human body. Yet, I have not personally tested its powers for the health of living creatures, and my commentaries do not go beyond my own experience.

However, in the refinement <note>original: *emendatione*, literally "correction" or "improvement" of metals</note> of metals, it displays singular powers. For I say, and clearly affirm, that whoever is able to bring the oil described here by Basil to the state of a fixed stone <term>fixed stone: a substance rendered stable and resistant to the heat of fire, a key stage in the Great Work</term> can already boast that he possesses a fixed tincture, second only to the King of Kings—the great Philosopher's Stone itself. When you have progressed this far, Friend of Chemistry <term>Philochymice: a term of endearment for a student or practitioner of alchemy</term>, do not turn back, nor take your hand from the plow: proceed with a joyful step, if by chance in so vast a forest there should appear that *Golden bough, with its leaves and pliant twig.* <note>original: *Aureus, & foliis, & lento vimine ramus* — A famous quote from Virgil’s *Aeneid*. Alchemists often used the "Golden Bough" as a metaphor for the hidden, divine substance needed for transmutation.</note>

<margin>A miracle of nature.</margin>
This is a great secret <term>arcanum magnum: a profound hidden mystery of alchemy</term>, which seems to contradict the laws of nature: that this oil first floats upon the surface, and afterwards, having been made heavier, seeks the bottom. Yet take note: oil of vitriol <term>oleum vitrioli: sulfuric acid</term> is also heavy, but since the mercury <note>In this context, "mercury" refers to a specific metallic principle extracted from antimony, not necessarily common quicksilver.</note> in its separation is not yet entirely pure, the oil stands above it. But when the "impure lightness" is stripped away from it by means of vinegar <term>acetum</term>—because the vinegar absorbs that lightness—then the oil receives its proper weight, becomes compact, and falls to the bottom. And this is the oil produced from the mercury of antimony, which is the fourth pillar <note>original: *columen*; referring to the foundational supports of medical practice</note> of medicine.

->## BASILIUS VALENTINUS<-

### The Triumph of Antimony

Indeed, if it please the Gods, you **Doctors** <note>Latin: *Doctores*; referring to the university-educated physicians and scholars of the era</note>, come to me, a religious man and a servant of God <note>The author, writing as Basil Valentine, presents himself as a Benedictine monk to emphasize his piety and divine inspiration</note>. I shall manifest to you that which your eyes have never seen; I shall show you the way of salvation and health <note>Latin: *salutis & sanitatis*; in alchemical philosophy, physical health was often seen as an extension of spiritual well-being</note> which until now you have not known. If, however, there be found among you anyone who understands my processes and the method of **preparation** <term>preparation|The specific laboratory procedures used to purify a mineral, such as antimony, to remove its poisonous qualities and activate its healing potential</term> better than I, let him, I pray, not be silent, nor place a lock upon his mouth. For I stand here ready to learn, and I am not ashamed to inquire further and to desire the light which perhaps I did not know before.

For my voice has already been heard quite often saying that our life is circumscribed by limits too narrow for a single man to investigate all that Nature carries hidden in her bosom. # The Triumph of Antimony

...you have received through training. Among many people, though they are unwise, this saying is frequently repeated: "fish are frozen in the waters." But these people show nothing but their own recklessness and ignorance; for it will never be proven that a fish is frozen in the water—even in the harshest winter—so long as the ice is daily opened by the diligence of men.

<margin>No animal can live without air.</margin>
The fact that fish die happens because, when the ice is not opened, the breathing of air is snatched away from them, and they are suffocated as a result. It can easily be proven that no animal can live when the inhalation of air is denied to it. Therefore, it is concluded that those fish which are found dead under the water during times of sharper cold are not killed by the cold itself—as the poorly informed suppose—but are extinguished by the denial of air. By the same logic,

<meta>catchword: as</meta>

<summary>The author uses a biological analogy to argue that air and respiration are essential for life, correcting the common belief that fish die from cold in frozen ponds when they actually die from suffocation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analogy of respiration introduced on the previous page. Having explained that fish die from lack of air rather than cold, the author now applies this concept to the extraction and preparation of antimony, emphasizing that it too requires "breathing" and labor to manifest its medicinal virtues.</meta>

->Basilius Valentinus<-

To apply this example, I say: if antimony <term>antimonium: a metallic element often used in alchemy and early medicine; while toxic in raw form, Valentinus believed it held supreme healing powers when purified</term> is to bring forth those incredible fruits, it must first be dug out from the mountains. From the very beginning, miners must laboriously create "breathing holes" <note>original: *respiratoria*</note> for it. Afterward, it must be prepared using water, air, and fire as helping tools, so that its inherent fertility is not suffocated in the earth. Instead, through the craftsman's many and difficult preparations, its power should be revealed and brought into the light for the hoped-for healing of diseases—the very purpose for which the seeker undertook his entire investigation.

Where are you now, you wretched soul! You who treat antimony as worthless and denounce it to everyone as nothing but pure poison—where is your Rhetoric or your Logic <note>original: *Dialectica*</note> to defend yourself? Since you understand neither the white, nor the black, nor the green, nor the red, nor the yellow <note>These colors refer to the *chromatic sequence* or stages of transformation in the alchemical process, indicating the progress of the "Great Work"</note>; and since you do not even know by what path you can approach...

### The Triumph of Antimony

...to <term>justify antimony<note>Latin: *justificare antimonium*. Antimony was a highly controversial mineral in the 16th and 17th centuries; while some saw it as a toxic poison, paracelsian alchemists viewed it as a potent medicine when "tamed" or purified.</note></term>, and even less are its virtue, power, and utility known to you, you do well to remain silent. You should allow this rebuke of your ignorance to fly over your head like a wave driven by a violent wind, fearing that, if those winds and waves should prevail, your fragile and leaky ship might seek the bottom and be submerged.

To avoid this danger, call out in good time to your sleeping Master, just as the disciples of our Savior Jesus Christ did when they feared they would perish. Do this not with a feigned or counterfeit heart, but from a true and pure one, free from all hypocrisy. Then, redemption and help will undoubtedly follow, so that you may see in all truth and observe the winds and the sea obeying you, allowing you to lead all things to the desired conclusion.

### Basil Valentine

Would that man were so minded that he might strive to attain something through labor and diligence; then certainly the gods of prosperity and the gods who preside over the Art <note>The "Art" refers to alchemy, specifically the "Great Work" of transforming matter and spirit.</note> would grant success. Through this success, such a disciple and follower of the Art would become certain that, in his desired <term>school of the art<note>Latin: *gymnasio artis*. This refers to the practical and philosophical training ground of the alchemist.</note></term> and his longed-for home of grace, happiness and health would not be denied him. 

Thus, he himself would surely see and find the foundation of the solid **cornerstone** <note>Latin: *lapis angularis*. In alchemy, this is a dual metaphor for both the Philosophers' Stone and the spiritual foundation of Christ.</note>, upon which the remaining rows of stones can be conveniently built. Then, so many ill-founded follies of babblers would cease, with which the ears of students in the schools and the heads of the sick in their homes are currently deafened. 

The matter itself, as if with an open mouth, will speak; and by certain experience, it will confirm that a **stone fortress** <note>Latin: *arx lapidea*. This represents the "fixed" or indestructible nature of a perfected alchemical substance that can withstand the test of fire.</note>, or palace, cannot be so easily ignited and consumed by fire as...

<meta>catchword: cor-</meta>

# The Triumphal Chariot of Antimony.

...wash their own. Are we not all, however many of us wretched mortals live here as pilgrims on earth, like smoke—possessing nothing that we can truly call our own? Are not all things we use the <term>feudal goods</term> <note>original: "feudalia bona"; property held in trust from a superior lord rather than owned outright, emphasizing that humans are stewards, not owners, of worldly things</note> of our Lord, granted to us only for as long as we live, and no longer? Let us therefore conduct ourselves in using these things in such a way that, at that time when both the account of them and the things themselves must be returned, we may stand firm, supported by a good conscience. Let us act so that we are not cast into prison and the outer darkness <note>original: "tenebras exteriores," a biblical reference to the state of being cast away from God</note> on account of our ingratitude, where there shall be "weeping and gnashing of teeth" <note>a common scriptural phrase describing the suffering and regret of those separated from the divine</note>.

If this were the subject of our meditation <note>original: "meditatio"; here meaning deep, focused contemplation on spiritual truths</note>, and if this were the intention of every person, it would be a strange marvel if anyone should think of admitting sin into themselves, or if anyone should think of neglecting their duty. Instead, everyone would strive eagerly to serve their neighbor with the gifts they have received from God...

# Basilius Valentinus

276

the plague itself, I have found it to be very beneficial.

<margin>Oil of antimony for fevers and the plague.</margin>
Antimony is ground finely, placed into a glass retort, and distilled with a strong fire without any other ingredients being added. This process is repeated a third or fourth time, always collecting the result in a large vessel; finally, it becomes a reddish powder. Extract <note>In alchemy, "extraction" involves using a solvent like vinegar to pull out the "essence" or active medicinal properties of a substance.</note> this powder with vinegar; let that extraction circulate over a gentle fire for exactly ten days. Remove the vinegar by distillation, and that which remains is to be transformed into an oil by distilling it through a unique <term>enchereſis: from the Greek "encheirēsis," meaning a manual operation or a specific "hand-work" technique required to finish a chemical process</term>. This oil is then further circulated until its humidity has been removed and it is reduced once more to a powder, just as it was when the vinegar was first separated from it by distillation at the beginning, and the final oil was collected in a fresh vessel.

<margin>Usage.</margin>
Four grains <note>A "grain" was the smallest unit of pharmaceutical weight, roughly 65 milligrams, used for potent medicines.</note> of this oil taken with water of Blessed Thistle <note>original: "aqua cardui Benedicti." This herb was a staple of the medieval garden, believed to cure anything from digestive issues to the bubonic plague.</note>

---

* This extraction must be made volatile <note>A "volatile" substance is one that easily evaporates. Alchemists sought to make their medicines volatile so they could "fly" through the body to reach the site of a disease.</note> with spirit of wine <term>spirit of wine: high-proof alcohol, often used as a solvent to carry the medicinal properties of minerals</term>, in the manner I taught in the previous operations.

# The Triumph of Antimony

...is made by means of a bell-glass <note>original: *campanam*; a bell-shaped vessel used in early chemistry to catch vapors</note>, a method of preparation that was not unknown to the ancients of long ago. However, take note that this way is superior: if you use an <term>alembic|a classic distillation apparatus consisting of a boiling vessel and a cooling cap with a spout</term> instead of a bell, and attach a receiving vessel to it, you will obtain a greater quantity of oil. It will be of the same color as the oil drawn from common sulfur, but in its power and virtue it is far more excellent than that.

<margin>Efficacy</margin>
We use it internally with <term>spirit of wine|high-proof alcohol or ethanol</term> in doses of three drops to treat <term>phthisis|a historical term for pulmonary tuberculosis or any wasting disease of the lungs</term> of the lungs. Externally, however, if it is applied as an ointment, and then...

* Now I shall speak to you through an illustration, not only in words, O <term>Philochymist|a lover of chemistry; from the Greek 'philos' (loving) and 'chymia' (chemistry)</term>, so that through this brief discourse you may learn how to save both labor and expense. Look at this instrument and construct it for yourself, so that you may follow <note>This refers to Basil Valentine, the legendary alchemist to whom this text is traditionally attributed.</note> Basil in making oil of sulfur by the bell method. For by this way, a single ounce will yield as much oil as a whole pound provides through the common, well-worn method. Indeed, from sixteen ounces of sulfur you will extract half an ounce of oil—a yield that others would scarcely hope to get from sixteen pounds.

### Basil Valentine

More would have to be written concerning this balsam, were I not afraid that some person unskilled in these matters, or some sophistical brawler <note>original: "rabula sophisticus," referring to a deceitful or loud-mouthed critic who uses clever but false arguments</note>, might attack me and claim that I am expanding upon these things too much and committing more to paper than experience has taught me—and therefore selling mere speculations and fantasies.

<margin>Oil of the Sulfur of antimony.</margin>
*Furthermore, another oil is prepared in this manner: Let one part of antimony be sublimated <term>sublimated: a process where a solid is heated until it turns into a vapor and then collects again as a solid on a cooler surface</term> with a fourth part of sal ammoniac <term>sal ammoniac: ammonium chloride, a salt used in alchemy to help lift or "volatize" metals</term> over a gentle fire. The salt carries the sulfur of the antimony upward, turning a deep red like blood. Grind this sublimate finely; and if you initially took one pound of antimony, grind it again with five more ounces of antimony and sublimate it once more, just as was done before. This sublimate is dissolved by placing it in a damp place <note>a process known as "deliquescence," where a substance absorbs enough moisture from the air to become liquid</note>. ---

<note>* This is another repetition of the process by which the **balsam of antimony** is prepared—as it is called in this treatise—or the **quintessence** <note>Latin: "quinta essentia," the purest and most concentrated essence of a substance</note> of antimony, which has been mentioned frequently above. However, there is this difference in the procedure: here, the sulfur is separated from the antimony by means of <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>Latin: "salem armoniacum," a salt used to help separate or sublimate minerals</note>, and then extracted with vinegar; whereas in other processes, the sulfur is extracted with vinegar while it is still joined to the antimony. But these differences are not so significant as to make the results of the operations doubtful. This variety, therefore, will allow the operator greater freedom, so that he is not overly anxious in those matters where he understands the logic of what he is doing and the method by which he acts.</note>

# Basil Valentine

<margin>The wonderful powers of this oil.</margin>
...in its own nature, without any corrosiveness or danger. It heals **consumption** <note>original: *phthisis*, a historical term for wasting diseases like tuberculosis</note>, dispels stitches in the side, and if anyone suffers from shortness of breath, let him take two grains in the morning and as many in the evening when he goes to bed, in an elixir or **spirit of wine** <note>original: *spiritus vini*, referring to distilled alcohol or ethanol</note>; it opens the tightness of the chest, expels all impurities and phlegm from the lungs, and has many times yielded for me effects that were completely unexpected.

Since, however, in other preparations of antimony I have described those powers which this oil shares with them, I do not think it necessary to repeat them all, fearing that I might cause boredom to those who pursue this art through **wordiness** <note>original: *perissologia*, a term derived from Greek meaning "superfluous speech"</note> or suggest distracting thoughts through foolish **repetition** <note>original: *tautologia*</note>. <page-num>186</page-num>

But because this matter is rare in its own right, it nonetheless usually happens through another way—of which we have already spoken—namely, that a **spirit** <note>original: *spiritus*, here referring to an assisting or demonic entity rather than a chemical vapor</note> assisting **astrologers** <note>original: *astrologis*</note> either illuminates or directs this power within their **imagination** <term>phantasia: in early modern psychology, the "imagination" was considered an internal sense that processed images and could be influenced by external spiritual forces</term> in this manner. It does this so that the mind finally arrives at a certain conclusion, just as they have at other times conducted a discourse in their own souls through other reasons or causes. 

This is all the more gathered from the fact that **astrology** is a thing that is exceedingly uncertain—uncertain both in the effects of the **planets** <note>original: *planetae*</note> and in the signs of the **stars** <note>original: *stellarum*</note>, as we have shown elsewhere. It is therefore no wonder if this very uncertainty, or the obscurity of astrology, is all the more suited to the spirit, so that it may insert itself through such a medium and draw the person along with false persuasions. Its goal is to finally conquer the person’s **freedom** <note>original: *libertatem*, referring to the theological concept of free will</note>, overcome the man, and lead him into **error**. 

From this it appears that this is almost another way in which astrology provides a medium through which astrologers arrive at foreign forms of knowledge. But because it is more fitting for a spirit to overcome a human being through indirect and hidden means, it almost always wishes to manifest itself in this way regarding these matters, as appears more clearly at the end of this stated chapter, and in section 84 and those following.

# Basil Valentine

...<term>sal ammoniac<note>Ammonium chloride, a salt used in alchemy to help "open" or dissolve metallic ores during heating.</note></term> at the beginning of the sublimation would have been better dissolved and opened <note>In alchemical terms, "opening" a body means breaking down its physical structure to release its inner essence or "spirit."</note>.

<margin>Whether the Mercury of the Philosophers can be found in antimony.</margin>
Furthermore, there remain many things to be written about antimony, and specifically three things which are necessary for the <term>Spagyric Physician<note>A practitioner of "Spagyrics," a branch of medicine following the teachings of Paracelsus that uses alchemical processes like fermentation and distillation to prepare remedies.</note></term> and Philosopher to know: namely, the preparation of the vinegar which is made from its ore; next, the **Philosophical signed star** <note>A star-like crystalline pattern that appears on the surface of purified antimony (regulus) as it cools; alchemists viewed this as a celestial "signature" or sign of the metal's purity.</note> must not be overlooked; and finally, a discourse must be held regarding the **Lead of the Philosophers**. Many have imagined great things about this "Lead" and have thought, through their own reasoning and speculation, that they could produce the true and sincere <term>Mercury of the Philosophers<note>The elusive, "living" essence of metals sought by alchemists to perform the Great Work of transmutation; distinct from common liquid mercury.</note></term> from it. However, this truly cannot be done, since such efficacy was not granted to antimony by heaven that this Mercury could exist within it or be prepared from it. Mercury is the first being, or the first water of metals, which

# The Triumph of Antimony

<margin>A specific property is found in antimony.</margin>
...which is perfect, from which the <term>Philosopher's Stone<note>original: "lapis magnus veterum sapientum," literally "the great stone of the ancient wise men." That <term>first essence<note>original: "ens primum," a term in alchemy for the primordial matter or the most basic, potent state of a substance.</note></term> and its seeds are found in another mineral in which the operation—following the nature of metals—is greater than in antimony. Inquire further into what pertains to internal medicine and what to external medicine; for antimony is the very pillar of every <term>apothecary's workshop<note>original: "Apothecariorum officinæ." # Basilius Valentinus

I will satisfy their desire by fulfilling my promise to them and by providing instruction, through which the true separation of good from evil will finally be revealed to them, and by providing information regarding the <term>Vinegar of the Philosophers<note>original: "aceto Philosophorum." This is not literal household vinegar, but a potent alchemical solvent believed to be essential for dissolving and purifying metals.</note></term>, which is prepared from antimony.

<margin>The Vinegar of the Philosophers from antimony.</margin>
*Let the mineral from which antimony is smelted and purified be ground most finely; place this material into a round glass vessel, which is called a <term>vial<note>original: "phiala."</note></term>, having a long neck. Let distilled rainwater be poured over it until the <term>cucurbit<note>original: "cucurbita." A gourd-shaped chemical vessel used as the lower part of a still.</note></term> is half full; once well sealed, let it be set aside in <term>horse dung<note>original: "fimo equino." In early chemistry, piles of fermenting horse manure were used as a reliable way to maintain a steady, gentle heat for weeks at a time.</note></term> so that it may <term>putrefy<note>The process of breaking down a substance through gentle heat and moisture, often seen as a necessary "death" before a substance could be reborn in a purified state.</note></term>, until the mineral begins to effervesce and gather foam on the surface. Then is the time to remove it, for it is a sign that the <term>body has opened itself<note>original: "corpus se aperuisse." This refers to the physical structure of the mineral breaking down to release its internal essence.</note></term>. Place this digested material into a cucurbit, seal it well, and draw off the water. It will have a somewhat acidic taste. When all the water has been removed, increase* <note>The text ends here; the catchword "ignem" (fire) from the original Latin indicates the next page begins with instructions to increase the heat of the furnace.</note>

# Basilius Valentinus

The powers of the Philosophers’ Vinegar.

Let the labor be resumed for three days and nights, and then repeated a third time; then your water will be acidic, like common vinegar. I have spoken as a Friend of Chemistry <note>original: "Philochymice"</note>; now you must act.*

# Basil Valentine

The powers of the Philosophers' vinegar.

This vinegar cools powerfully—not like common vinegar, but with great wonder and proven experience—especially for soothing gangrene produced by <term>gunpowder</term> <note>original: "pulvere pyrio"; literally "fire powder."</note>. # The Triumph of Antimony

<margin>
How the marked star of antimony is made.
</margin>
...two parts; one part of *steel*: these are melted with four parts of *burnt tartar* <term>burnt tartar: potassium carbonate, a salt produced by heating the sediment found in wine barrels; used here as a flux to help metals melt</term>, and are poured into an iron bowl, such as goldsmiths use when they purify their gold. When it has cooled, remove the *regulus* <term>regulus: the "little king," referring to the pure metallic button that settles at the bottom of a crucible after smelting</term>, and separate it from all impurity and *slag* <note>original: "scoria"</note>. Grind this *regulus* finely, check its weight, and add to it three times its weight of *burnt tartar*; melt and pour it out as before, and repeat this a third time. Then the *regulus* purifies itself and comes out pure and bright. Note, however, that when you have correctly performed the melting and have used the proper *manual skill* <note>original: "encherefi," derived from the Greek "encheiresis," meaning the specialized "hand-work" or physical technique required by the chemist</note> which is the masterpiece of this work, you will obtain a beautiful star, and from white

> \* It must be noted here: In the third melting of the *regulus*, the fire must be strongly intensified so that by that intense heat, any impurity still residing in the *regulus* may be removed. Thus you will achieve a *regulus* comparable to silver in beauty and whiteness, but far superior in power and value.

### Basilius Valentinus

# The Triumph of Antimony. 301
N 7

...they produce a certain [substance] and cast it off from themselves, which is in no way similar to their fruit in either form or strength, but possesses entirely different properties; this is seen in **oak trees** and **apple trees**, which produce similar illegitimate fruits or monsters <note>In early modern natural philosophy, "monsters" referred to any biological anomaly or growth that deviated from the typical form of the species, such as galls on an oak tree.</note>. In the same way, the earth also has similar miscarriages, which are separated and cast out during the division from pure metals.

Although there is now such a close kinship between <term>antimony</term> <note>A brittle, silvery-white metalloid</note> and <term>Saturn</term> <note>original: "saturno" — the alchemical name for the metal Lead</note>, antimony was nonetheless cast out by him because of the excessive <term>sulfur</term> <note>In alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, masculine principle of heat and combustibility.</note> it contains. This occurred because the abundance of hot sulfur caused its <term>mercury</term> <note>The principle of liquidity and metallic character</note>—due to a lack of the cold by which it is usually driven back—to be unable to reach a state of <term>coagulation</term> <note>The hardening or solidification of a substance</note> or to develop into a malleable body...

# APPENDIX

In place of a conclusion, you should know that antimony is employed for many more uses than just the type <note>original: scripturam</note> which printers <term>Typographers|Early printers used an alloy of lead, tin, and antimony for movable type because antimony expands as it cools, ensuring sharp letters</term> use. Also, under a certain specific constellation and conjunction of the planets, a mixture of metals is made with antimony, from which seals <note>original: signaturæ</note> and characters are cast, endowed with singular virtues <note>In alchemy and astrology, "virtues" refers to inherent powers or spiritual properties</note>. From this same mixture, mirrors <note>original: specula; likely referring to "magic mirrors" used for divination or experimental optics</note> are also fashioned, possessing many wonderful appearances and properties.

->The Works of Basil Valentine<-

Furthermore, hand-bells and church-bells of an admirable sound are made from it. Also figures of men and many other things: *

* The powers and virtues of antimony, which the author touches upon in this appendix only in passing <note>original: tanquam per transennam; literally "as if through a lattice," a Latin idiom for a brief or superficial glance</note> and then skips over, are so numerous and varied that not even a hundredth part of them is yet known to mankind. This ignorance is undoubtedly a disgrace and a shame to our race, for we humans—who alone among so many living creatures are endowed with the power of reason and the ability to think—are driven with such force by that "sacred" and abominable greed for gain <note>original: sacram illam atque abominabilem lucri cupiditatem; an ironic reference to the "cursed hunger for gold"</note> that hardly anyone has the time to investigate those wonders which the Author of Nature <note>A common reference to God as the creator of the natural laws</note> has placed within His creatures. But I do not wish to repeat this lament so often sung by Valentine; only reflect on this: that this mineral, in which such an admirable spirit lies hidden that it cannot be exhausted by being drawn out, also possesses powers which no one can fully grasp through study alone. What I have personally experienced outside of <term>Chemistry|Chymia; in the 17th century, this term referred to the inseparable practices of alchemy and early chemistry</term> and Medicine are few; yet experience has taught me that they are of such a kind that I do not believe antimony will prove itself less wonderful in other matters than it has already shown itself to be in Chemistry and Medicine.

# The Triumph of Antimony

...held by the desire of imitating <note>original: "imitandi... desiderio"; likely referring to the alchemist's goal of imitating Nature’s creative processes</note>, have handed down. <margin>All metals and minerals take their origin from the Stars.</margin>
Indeed, I find—to explain the matter in few words—that all things generated in the bowels of the mountains are infused by the stars above. They take their beginning from a watery mist, smoke, or moist vapor; over a great length of time, these are nourished by the stars and brought into a tangible form by the elements <note>The "four elements" (Fire, Air, Water, Earth) were considered the building blocks of all physical matter in Western alchemy</note>. Furthermore, this vapor is dried so that the watery quality loses its dominance, and fire (with the help of air) takes control. Thus, fire is produced from water, and earth is produced from fire and air. These elements are still found together in all things that possess a physical body, prior to their separation...

# Basil Valentine <note>original: "Basilii Valentini"; referring to the legendary 15th-century alchemist, though these texts are often attributed to later authors writing under his name.</note>

<margin>The first matter of all things is water.</margin>
...are found. This, therefore, is the * first matter of all things—namely, water—which has been formed into earth through the drying action of fire and air. 

Now, since it is certain to me that I must describe the stone which is called *fire* <note>original: "ignis"; here referring to the "Stone of Fire" (*Lapis Ignis*), a specific alchemical preparation made from antimony</note>, and which is made from antimony, together with the process of its preparation—since it heals not only human beings but specifically metals as well,

---

<note>The following section explains the fundamental philosophy behind the alchemical process.</note>

* This is an old song, this is the summit of the art; it was discovered through this imitation of nature, and from this it is made, if ever this lesser stone of fire is produced: from the same source, that great Philosophical Stone is prepared. This is the water of Anaxagoras <note>Anaxagoras (c. 500–428 BCE) was a Greek philosopher who taught that all things are composed of infinitely divisible "seeds"</note>, the fire of Empedocles <note>Empedocles (c. 494–434 BCE) originated the theory of the four classical elements</note>, and the first matter <term>materia prima<gloss>The formless, fundamental substance from which all physical reality was thought to emerge</gloss></term> of Aristotle, from which all things were made and are still made to this day. This is clearly evident in the nutrition of humans, the growth of trees, and the generation of metals: for it is not taken from food, rain, or earth alone, but is infused into these from elsewhere, constituting flesh, wood, and metals. It is that very nourishment which feeds all things; but for it to be specified in such various ways, it must be separated from the body in which it dwells and joined to another, which is accomplished by the Chemical art <note>The "Chemical art" refers here to alchemy, the practice of purifying and transforming substances</note>.

# The Triumph of Antimony. 315

O 2

<margin>The stone of fire tinges only partially.</margin>
However, the <term>stone of fire</term> <note>original: "Lapis ignis"; a potent alchemical substance described by Basil Valentine, considered secondary to the Great Work.</note> does not <term>tinge</term> <note>In alchemy, to "tinge" is to permanently change the color and nature of a base metal into a noble one.</note> universally, as does the * <term>Philosopher's stone</term>, which is made from the very essence of gold itself. By no means; this power has not been granted to it, to achieve such great feats, but it tinges only partially: namely, transforming silver <note>original: "lunam"</note> into gold <note>original: "solem"</note>, as well as tin and lead; however, it does not affect iron <note>original: "martem"</note> and copper <note>original: "venerem"</note>, nor do they yield more than can be produced from them through <term>separation</term> <note>A chemical process of isolating components, rather than a total transmutation of the substance's soul.</note>. Furthermore, one part of this tincture cannot transmute more than five parts; which remain in lead <note>original: "saturno"</note> and antimony itself: whereas, on the contrary, the great stone of the Philosophers in—

---

* Just as the heaven is elevated above the earth, so far does the true stone of the Philosophers differ from this stone of fire. I myself candidly confess that, although I have found this one, I am a whole heaven's distance from that one. And this knowledge, whatever it may be, I attribute to the wisdom of <term>Basil</term> <note>Referring to Basil Valentine, the purported author of this text, whose name translates to "The Mighty King."</note>. Take care that you are not led astray by your own fantasy and lose your senses, deceiving both yourself and others.

<summary>The author distinguishes the "stone of fire" (antimony-based) from the "Philosopher's Stone" (gold-based), noting that the former is limited in its power to transmute only certain metals like silver, tin, and lead. The text humbles the practitioner, attributing all knowledge to Basil Valentine and warning against self-deception in alchemical pursuits.</summary>

# Basil Valentine 316

...can achieve infinite results: even in its <term>augmentation</term> <note>The alchemical process of increasing the quantity or potency of a substance.</note> it cannot be so highly <term>exalted</term> <note>Purified or raised to a higher state of spiritual or physical perfection.</note>; nevertheless, the resulting gold <note>original: "aurum"</note> is pure and solid.

<margin>Various particular tinctures.</margin>
The reader should take note, however, that many kinds of "stones" are found which <term>tinge</term> <note>To permanently transform the color and nature of a base metal.</note> <term>partially</term> <note>original: "particulariter"; referring to "particular" transmutations that only work on specific metals, unlike the "universal" stone.</note>. I refer to all <term>fixed</term> <note>Substances that remain stable and do not evaporate or change when exposed to heat.</note> powders that tinge by the name of "stones" here. Yet one tinges more deeply than another: first and foremost is the <term>Philosopher's Stone</term>, which leads the procession; then follows the tincture of gold <note>original: "solis"</note> and silver <note>original: "lunæ"</note>, etc., for the white work; then the tincture of vitriol or copper <note>original: "veneris"</note>, and likewise the tincture of iron <note>original: "martis"</note>, both of which contain the tincture of gold within themselves, provided they are brought to fixation—

# The Writings of Basil Valentine

### 318

...they are conducive <note>original: "conducunt"</note>: but for metallic works they are truly useless, and are devoid of all power: the **Philosopher’s Stone** <term>Philosopher's Stone: the ultimate goal of alchemy, believed to be a substance that could turn base metals into gold and grant eternal life or perfect health</term> embraces all of their virtues—those of minerals, animals, and vegetables alike—all gathered into one.

<margin>Salts *are the keys of the tinctures.*</margin>
Truly, salts are endowed with no power of dyeing <note>original: "tingendi"; in alchemy, this refers to the "tincture" or the ability to change the color and nature of a metal</note>, but are only \* keys for the preparation of the stones; otherwise, as far as they themselves are concerned, they can effect nothing. Howe-

---

\* Keys, as is rightly said here, are the salts; they open the chest in which the treasure lies hidden, though they do not constitute the treasure themselves. One must see to it, however, that you take the true key, lest you damage the lock and fail to open the chest. In this matter, it is not safe to take *one thing for another* <note>original: "quid pro quo"; a common pharmaceutical term for substituting one ingredient for another</note> after the manner of the apothecaries. It is necessary that the key be a philosophical one, and that specific salts suited for opening be taken; nor should you despise that distinction which is suggested between the **opening salts**—which he calls "keys" here—and the **fixing salts**, which enter into the treasure itself, as is clear enough from the text.
